Carlsberg · CARL.B-DK · the new Memo conveyor's card of 28 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Carlsberg

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 50,456 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

F3632nd of 36

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external environment is a moderate headwind, and the Chinese consumer is its swing factor. China has turned down sharply in the last six months: Q2 volume fell 6% and Q3 about 10%, in the quarters that earn about two-thirds of China's profit. Peers' data show that part of the loss is share and channel, not only weather. Western European beer is in structural decline. Mainstream competition has turned to price in the UK and Poland on rule-driven triggers that will persist. Regulation and 2027 input costs add a margin drag that needs extra price.

The tailwinds are company-specific or mechanical:

  • PepsiCo licences;
  • alcohol-free beer;
  • India;
  • easy Chinese comparatives in Q2-Q3 2027;
  • a mild currency tailwind.

None of these is a demand inflection. For the Consumer: the model should not rely on demand. Volume should be flat to +1% underlying, price and mix 2-3%, and China treated as a range (2027 Chinese volume -3% to +3%), with the risk that weakness persists.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0235C-0538C-0738C-0954C-3578C-3952
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

F3530th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Competitive intensity is tightening, towards price-for-volume in mainstream lager, in three of Carlsberg's important markets (the UK, Poland and China), and easing in Vietnam. Carlsberg's response is to concede mainstream volume to protect margin. That protects profit, but it caps volume and keeps the "false friend" debate alive: the risk that soft-drink growth hides beer erosion.

Why:

  • UK. Heineken cut the alcohol strength of Foster's and spent the duty saving on price cuts; Molson Coors followed on Carling. Carlsberg Danish Pilsner lost on-trade share. The trigger is a duty rule based on alcohol strength, so the pressure is durable, not a passing tactic.
  • Poland. Asahi discounted mainstream lager into the disruption from the deposit return scheme. Carlsberg's volume fell by low double digits in H1, and an industry expert expects Carlsberg to become more price-aggressive.
  • China. Heineken, through China Resources Beer, is out-executing in premium. AB InBev is investing in off-trade distribution. 1664 Blanc faces frequent price competition.
  • Vietnam. The Sabeco-Heineken price war has abated, and Carlsberg's Huda brand recovered.
  • What lies ahead. Heineken's new chief executive starts on 1 October 2026 under the EverGreen 2030 strategy:
  • EUR 400-500m a year of savings, much of it reinvested;
  • marketing above 10% of revenue against Carlsberg's 8.3%.

AB InBev is reinvesting in the US and China. This raises the bar on marketing intensity.

  • Q3 against peers. Heineken grew organic revenue 4.6% on volume of +3.9% (Asia Pacific +17.3%). AB InBev grew 4.9% on flat volume. Carlsberg grew 3.0% on flat volume. Peers are taking share in Asia.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

F3827th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Neutral in 2026: cost of sales per hectolitre is flattish under a hedge of about 90%. A moderate headwind in 2027: Morgan Stanley forecasts input cost inflation of 5.4%, and the Industry digest records a headwind of about 160bp. The upside tail is fatter now that oil is at USD 111 and aluminium is near its post-Ukraine highs. Can and glass suppliers are highly concentrated, but Carlsberg's central procurement and Britvic's scale offset that. Net: a headwind from 2027 that needs about 1-1.5% of extra price.

Why:

  • Hedging:
  • At least 70% of barley and aluminium is hedged by Q3 of the prior year, and 90% at the start of the year.
  • Western European energy is hedged on a rolling 15-month basis.
  • Nordic power purchase agreements fix electricity prices for up to 10 years.
  • Utilities in Asia and CEEI cannot be hedged.
  • Sensitivities:
  • A 10% rise in aluminium needs about 1.3% of price to hold gross margin (Bernstein).
  • A 10% rise in energy needs about 1% of price (Jefferies).
  • Cans are 36-38% of volume; packaging is 40-50% of brewing cost; raw materials are about 25% of cost of goods sold.
  • Offsets. Barley and sugar are tailwinds. Barley is at 46% of its 2022 peak, and malting barley for the 2026 harvest is up only 3%.
  • Peer evidence:
  • China Resources Beer's beer gross margin fell 1.0 point on aluminium.
  • Tsingtao's Q3 margin fell as aluminium rose 17% and corrugated paper 19%.
  • Tail risk. Oil at USD 111 is above the USD 100 trigger named in the Fitness for Fighting digest. Jefferies' stress case, oil at USD 145, raises cost of goods sold by 25%.
  • Policy. Price in line with local inflation, and cover more than 80% of input inflation through revenue management.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

D3817th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The macro bites hardest at company level, through Carlsberg's China weight and its emerging-market currencies:

  • China is 30% of its beer volume, against 3% at Heineken and 13% at AB InBev.
  • Non-krone currencies are 92% of revenue.

At sector level, the macro is currently a relative tailwind for valuation: the US 10-year yield is near 5.1% and markets are risk-off, so investors are rotating into defensives. At industry level, the squeeze on discretionary spending in Europe and China is a volume headwind.

Why:

  • China. Consumers planning to spend more on discretionary items fell to a one-year low of 46%. The property slump continues, Q3 GDP is about 4.3%, and austerity weighs. Management cannot separate weather from weak consumers.
  • Europe. UK households are shifting spending to essentials. Poland and Germany are price-sensitive. On-trade footfall is weak.
  • Currencies:
  • Revenue mix: sterling 21%, renminbi 14%, euro 18% (pegged to the krone), emerging-market currencies 18%.
  • Translation cut H1 revenue by 1.3%.
  • FY2026 translation on operating profit is now +DKK 100m.
  • A 5% currency move is worth about DKK 200m of operating profit.
  • Forecasts have the renminbi rising to 6.70-6.75 per US dollar, a mild tailwind into 2027.
  • Rates:
  • Net finance costs are guided at DKK 1.8bn.
  • 18% of gross debt must be refinanced by 2028.
  • Refinancing 12 months of maturities at 100bp higher cuts pre-tax profit by 0.8% (Morgan Stanley).
  • Sentiment. In October the brewers rose 9.6-17.4% on defensive flows. Carlsberg's average target price fell 0.2% while its price rose 14.1% (Industry digest). The macro is currently lifting the multiple, not the earnings.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Three dynamics matter:

  1. Weather. The 2026 Chinese floods created easy comparatives for Q2-Q3 2027. This is positive and mechanical.
  2. PepsiCo's re-allocation of bottling licences. It makes Carlsberg the anchor bottler, which is positive. It also shows that licences move: - Carlsberg lost the UK San Miguel licence in 2025, a drag of 2-3% on EBIT. - Its Coca-Cola contracts in Denmark and Finland end in 2028. - Royal Unibrew loses 13% of its sales to Carlsberg in 2029.
  3. The sector rotation. It lifted the shares without estimate support and can reverse quickly. The Fitness for Fighting digest set its Momentum tilt to Underweight because defensives have already led.

Why:

  • Geopolitics. Ukraine is about 3% of revenue, and its volume fell by low double digits. Middle East energy risk and Laotian hyperinflation are the others.
  • Sell-side positioning. Jefferies downgraded the stock to Hold on 13 September. DNB Carnegie's Daniel Andersen kept a trading sell on Chinese volume falling 7-9% (9 October).
  • Sentiment sequence. Heineken's and China Resources Beer's results come before Carlsberg's and move the read on China.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

A781st of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. The overall threat is low.

Type Threat Evidence
Standard entrants Very low Brewing capital, brand spend, locked-up distribution, advertising bans and state excise and permit regimes block them
Adjacent entrants Low to medium Spirits-based ready-to-drink cocktails (big in the US, a European sideshow per Bernstein); soft-drink bottlers in Carlsberg's new categories (Coca-Cola Europacific Partners, Coca-Cola HBC, and Royal Unibrew's own brands after 2029, possibly with a Nordic Coca-Cola partnership); milk tea and other drinks in China
Orthogonal entrants Medium, slow Digital B2B marketplaces (AB InBev's BEES distributes third-party non-beer); instant retail in China (1% to 6% by 2027); private label in European grocery

The most material is private label combined with retailer power in Europe.

C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

C5213th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Where relevant, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

Judgement. Both:

  • Raw-material and packaging suppliers are a risk in 2027: aluminium, energy, glass, and a concentrated can and glass supply base.
  • Carlsberg is a strong buyer. Procurement is centralised in Carlsberg Supply Company in Switzerland, Britvic added scale, and it uses should-cost models and value engineering (for example, PET bottles cut from 22.5g to 18.5g in Denmark).
  • Its payables show buyer power: about 201 days outstanding, and supplier finance of DKK 3,171m with terms up to 180 days, up from 140 days. That supplier finance is also a hidden source of cash that would reverse if the programmes were curtailed.
  • PepsiCo, as concentrate supplier and brand owner, is supportive:
  • ten-year rolling agreements;
  • co-funded marketing;
  • repeated awards of territory.

But it controls the economics through its concentrate pricing.

Why it matters for the model:

  • Working capital. Supplier finance and long payables are why trade working capital is negative. Assume no further benefit, and a 0.5-1 point of revenue downside risk.
  • Supply disruption. Can shortages in India in 2026 forced costlier alternatives.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0135C-0345C-0438C-0834C-3242C-3736C-4344
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

F3534th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. End-market demand is weakening on balance. Beer is about two-thirds of volume, and its demand is flat to declining structurally. China is cyclically weak and may be structurally weak. Soft drinks, alcohol-free beer, India and Vietnam are growing. Over the last six months the weakening was concentrated in China, where volume fell 6% in Q2 and about 10% in Q3. Comparatives become easy only from Q2 2027, so Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 should stay soft. Visibility is low for China and medium elsewhere. No part of demand qualifies as a cyclical swing with two or more years of visibility.

Why:

  • Beer. Global beer volume is forecast to grow 0.2% a year to 2030. Carlsberg's footprint embeds 0.3% a year, the lowest of the major brewers (Heineken's is 1.0%). In H1 2026, Western European beer fell 3.9% while soft drinks there grew 3.2%. Group beer volume fell 1.0% while non-beer grew 7.8%.
  • China. China is about 30% of beer volume and about 14% of revenue. Q2 and Q3 together carry about 60% of China's sales and about 66% of its profit, so the weak summer hits full-year China profit hard. Peers show where the weakness sits:
  • Heineken's licensed volume through China Resources Beer grew in the high twenties.
  • China Resources Beer itself grew 1.7% in H1.
  • Budweiser APAC fell 9.7% in Q2.

The weakness is therefore in on-trade, nightlife and super-premium channels, where Carlsberg and AB InBev are over-indexed (1664 Blanc fell 5% in Q3). It is partly share and channel, not only weather.

  • Growth categories. Soft drinks, premium beer and alcohol-free beer are 51% of volume:
  • Soft drinks grew 9% organically in H1, or 4% excluding Kazakhstan.
  • Alcohol-free beer grew 11%.
  • India grew in the mid-teens and Vietnam more than 20% in H1.
  • The improvement in volume came from a licence, not from demand. Organic volume was -2.0% in FY2025, +1.7% in H1 2026 and +0.3% in Q3 2026. The Kazakhstan licence added 1-1.5 points and laps in 2027. In the terms of the Wisdom Library's "Use, Refill or Price" test, the lift came from the perimeter, not from use.
  • Implication for the model. Underlying group volume is flat to +1% a year. China volume falls 5-8% for FY2026. FY2027 China volume is flat to +3% on comparatives alone, provided the share loss is not structural.
C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

D4525th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The disruption forces are strengthening slowly: moderation and alcohol-free beer, ready-to-drink cocktails, digital ordering platforms, weight-loss drugs and private label. Carlsberg is on the right side of the biggest force in its footprint, because through alcohol-free beer and soft drinks it owns part of the substitute. It is on the wrong side of digital ordering platforms: its Servd platform is a tool for its own ordering, while AB InBev's BEES and Heineken's eazle are monetised marketplaces. Net direction: mildly negative for beer volume and mildly positive for portfolio mix. This is not a thesis-breaker within 18 months.

Why:

  • Alcohol-free beer. It grew 11% in H1 2026 (15% in Western Europe) and is 7% of Western European beer volume. It pays no beer duty, so it can earn more gross profit per litre, which turns moderation into a gain in mix. That gain builds slowly.
  • Moderation. 53% of UK adults now buy low- or no-alcohol drinks monthly, against 40% in 2023. About half of Gen Z drinkers in Europe want to drink less, and weight-loss (GLP-1) drug users drink less.
  • Ready-to-drink cocktails. These grow about 20% a year in the US but are a sideshow in Europe. Carlsberg has no US exposure, which helps.
  • Digital ordering platforms:
  • AB InBev's BEES carries 72% of its revenue.
  • Heineken's eazle serves about 800,000 customers.
  • Carlsberg's Servd and One Metric cover 29 markets and 6,000 sales representatives, but Carlsberg lags in third-party marketplace income.
  • China channel shift. Instant-retail delivery of alcohol in China is forecast to rise from 1% in 2023 to 6% by 2027. On-premise's share of Chinese beer fell from 51.5% in 2019 to 47.5% in 2025, eroding the high-margin on-trade where Carlsberg's premium brands sell.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F3828th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Regulatory change is dense and sequenced, and it mostly pushes cost and price against the company. The norm is that it gets passed through to prices, with a lag. The largest single exposure is Vietnamese excise. The only material positive is Indian state excise reform (Karnataka). Within 18 months this is a margin drag of tens of basis points and a volume drag in Poland, the UK and Vietnam, not a structural break.

Why:

  • Vietnam:
  • Special consumption tax on beer rises from 65% in 2026 to 90-100% by 2030-31, about 5 points a year from 2027.
  • Carlsberg holds about 8% volume share; prices must rise about 3% a year; market growth slows to 2-3% (Rothschild & Co Redburn).
  • Decree 90 brings fines; Carlsberg itself was fined in May 2026 for misleading claims about Huda.
  • Packaging:
  • Poland's deposit return scheme from January 2026 caused double-digit volume falls through destocking.
  • The UK scheme starts 1 October 2027; Redburn expects a 12-month slowdown in UK soft drinks.
  • The EU packaging regulation applies from August 2026.
  • Austria's can deposit cut the can market 30-40% (Heineken), a precedent.
  • Sugar:
  • Poland from July 2026, a mid-teens effect on price.
  • Italy from January 2026.
  • The UK threshold falls to 4.5g per 100ml on 1 January 2028, bringing about 8% more of the market into scope. Regular Pepsi contains 4.5g; the focus on Pepsi Max limits the exposure.
  • Other:
  • Malaysian excise rose 10% in Budget 2026.
  • India runs 28 state regimes; Karnataka's alcohol-content tax lowered beer prices against spirits.
  • The Russian seizure of Baltika in July 2023 cost DKK 47.7bn, the precedent for political tail risk.
  • Incumbents. The claim that the rules entrench incumbents (Redburn) is argued, not shown.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F3435th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. No sector-level driver is accelerating Carlsberg's demand:

  • Premiumisation is the industry's value driver: category value grows 3.4% a year, of which about 3.2 points is price and mix. In Carlsberg's footprint it is slowing. Premium volume grew 5% in 2025 (excluding San Miguel), 3% in Q1 2026 and 1% in H1 2026, and Chinese super-premium is falling.
  • The drivers that are accelerating are alcohol-free beer, soft drinks and Indian beer. They are company participation, not a market theme, and too small to lift group demand by more than about a point a year.
  • The one structural driver is PepsiCo's re-allocation of bottling licences. It accelerates Carlsberg's volume through perimeter, not demand: the PepsiCo footprint grows from 5 markets in 2024 to 17 by January 2029.

Why:

  • India. The beer category grows 4.8% a year and Carlsberg in the mid-teens. But on our inference India is only about 3-4% of group EBITDA: an IPO valuation of about USD 4bn at roughly 33x EBITDA implies EBITDA of about USD 120m. India is a valuation and deleveraging lever, not an 18-month earnings driver.
  • Soft drinks. Carlsberg's soft drinks grew 4% excluding Kazakhstan, against Britvic's five-year volume growth of 1.5%. Category growth is mid-single digit in value.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

F4233rd of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Customer demand supports the profit half of the thesis and not the revenue half. The support is fragile in China and in mainstream lager.

Why:

  • Supportive:
  • Premium holds outside China: the Carlsberg brand grew 6% globally and 16% in Western Europe in H1.
  • Soft drinks grew 9% and alcohol-free beer 11%.
  • India grew in the mid-teens and Vietnam more than 20%.
  • Large retailers prefer fewer, larger suppliers in soft economies (Deutsche Bank).
  • Unsupportive:
  • Mainstream beer is eroding: Chongqing and Wusu brands in China, Carlsberg Danish Pilsner in the UK, Poland.
  • Super-premium has hit its price limit in China (1664 Blanc).
  • Consumption is shifting from on-trade to the home: Chinese on-premise fell from 51.5% of beer in 2019 to 47.5% in 2025, and Millennial bar visits are down in Europe.
  • Millennials and Gen Z, 32% of spend, are a long-run headwind to how often people drink.
  • Fragility. Weather swings Chinese volume by 5-10% in the peak quarters. Moderation is slow but one-directional.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

F3630th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. On balance they hurt, through excise, sugar levies, packaging schemes and marketing limits (detailed in C-04). Pass-through is the norm but comes with a lag and a volume cost. The position is fragile to sudden escalation in Vietnam and to a possible European ban on alcohol advertising (France, Spain and Belgium are pushing). That would hamper even the promotion of low-alcohol alternatives.

Why:

  • Competition authorities. The UK competition authority's phase-one review of Britvic (2024) was cleared. Legacy fines: India 2021, Germany EUR 50m in 2023, Super Bock EUR 57m under appeal.
  • Political tail risk. The Russian seizure in 2023 (DKK 47.7bn). Ukraine and Kazakhstan exposure is mitigated by hard-currency deposits held within the group.
  • The only clear help. Indian state reforms that tax by alcohol content, and the extra compliance burden that small rivals cannot carry, which Redburn argues entrenches incumbents.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F4433rd of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. The market is large but slow. Global beer is 1.86bn hectolitres and USD 193bn of revenue (2024). Volume grows 0.2% a year to 2030 and value 3.4% a year, of which about 3.2 points is price and mix. Carlsberg's footprint embeds 0.3% beer volume growth. On top of that come soft drinks (mid-single-digit value growth in its European and Central Asian markets) and India (category volume +4.8% a year; Carlsberg in the mid-teens). Together these allow organic revenue growth of about 3-4% a year, and about 4-5% reported including new PepsiCo territories.

Why:

  • The target needs top-quartile execution. Delivery since the 4-6% target was set: 2.7% in H1 2026 and 3.0% in Q3. The ten-year organic compound rate is 3.1% (2015-25).
  • Share gains are available but slow. Carlsberg's revenue is about 7% of global beer value, counting its soft drinks.
  • For the model. Use 3.5-4% organic revenue growth for FY2027-28 (consensus 3.8%), with PepsiCo licences and acquisitions as reported-only extras.
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0650C-1034
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

F5027th of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. The cycle is turning up slowly in revenue, not in volume. Organic revenue was +2.7% in H1 2026 and +3.0% in Q3, but the turn is in price and mix and in the licence volume from Kazakhstan, not in underlying volume, and China is in a down-leg. Profit is further through its cycle than revenue: organic operating profit grew 5.0% in FY2025 and 5.9% in H1 2026. 2027 brings mechanical support:

  • easy Chinese comparatives;
  • the Azerbaijan licence;
  • higher margin in Kazakhstan.

It also brings a mechanical drag: Kazakhstan's volume laps.

Why:

  • Volume. -2.0% in FY2025 (-0.6% excluding the San Miguel exit), +1.7% in H1 2026 (of which Kazakhstan was 1-1.5 points), and +0.3% in Q3.
  • Price and mix:
  • +0.8% in Q1 and +1.0% in H1, then about +2.7-3% in Q3.
  • Part of the Q3 acceleration is geographic mix. On our arithmetic, Chinese revenue per hectolitre is about 30% below the group average, so a 10% fall in Chinese volume adds roughly 0.5-1 point to the group's revenue per hectolitre.
  • Read H2 as a whole before concluding that pricing power has re-accelerated.
  • Implication. Model the 2027 volume lap from Kazakhstan (-1 to -1.5 points of group volume) separately from its margin gain (in-house production replaces toll-filling, the bottling done by third parties under contract).
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

F3433rd of 35

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement. Headwinds outweigh tailwinds. The external picture is a moderate headwind, and stronger than 12-18 months ago because China turned down, mainstream competition tightened in two markets and 2027 costs are rising. This is why the thesis has to be self-help.

Driver Direction Note
Beer volume and moderation (sector) Headwind Structural
Premiumisation (sector) Tailwind Fading in China, holding in Europe
Multi-beverage and soft drinks (cohort) Tailwind Mostly through licences
Alcohol-free beer Tailwind Small
Input costs Neutral in 2026, headwind in 2027
Regulation Headwind Steady
Chinese consumer Headwind The largest swing
Currencies Mild tailwind in 2026-27
Rates and defensive rotation Valuation tailwind Fragile

By level:

  • Value chain. Customers and competitors are negative, suppliers neutral to negative, and the brand owners that license to Carlsberg (PepsiCo, Sapporo) positive.
  • Industry. Concentrated and rational at group level.
  • Sector. Rated near decade lows and now bid as defensives.

Implication for the model. Assume:

  • underlying volume flat to +1%;
  • price and mix of 2-3% in 2027, linked to local inflation and covering about 160bp of cost headwind;
  • little help from demand.

Profit growth must come from Britvic synergies, Kazakhstan, pricing and lower finance costs.

Internal change forces/risks

B678th of 36

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The internal forces are the investment case: broad, resourced and largely delivered on the lines management controls:

  • organic operating profit +5.0% in FY2025 and +5.9% in H1 2026;
  • synergies ahead of plan;
  • leverage from 3.9x to 3.0x;
  • the Sapporo sale at 21.3x EBIT;
  • the Kazakhstan plant on schedule.

Leadership is stable and aligned, and the controlling Foundation supports continuity. The forces that remain negative are internal too:

  • below-the-line leakage from the hybrid coupon and the Sapporo minority;
  • earnings quality in transition;
  • a cost culture now paid to grow revenue it has not yet delivered.

The forces that would surprise positively are the buyback restart, Kazakhstan's margin step in 2027, and an India listing. The judgement for the Consumer: internal change supports profit growth of 5-7% a year and a buyback from about mid-2027. It does not support the revenue target.

Internal risks

Group judgement

Risks

C-4217th of 35

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The core growth engine is slowing:

  • premium beer volume growth fell from 5% to 1%;
  • 1664 Blanc is falling;
  • China fell 6% in Q2 and about 10% in Q3.

Revenue growth runs below the company's normal rate, and the input change forces (synergies, the Kazakhstan boost) mature in 2026-27. Sudden fundamental risks are concentrated in China, in energy and packaging costs at USD 111 oil, and in political exposure (Ukraine, Kazakhstan, Laos). Sentiment risk is elevated: the October rerating came without estimate support and has begun to reverse. Downside to a fair trough multiple is 10-20% on unchanged earnings, against upside of about 10-12% to the median target. The risk-reward has narrowed since the memo.

For the Consumer: build a downside case around three things together:

  • 2027 Chinese volume flat to down again;
  • price and mix below 2% against input inflation above 5%;
  • a buyback delayed to 2028.

On that case, FY2027 EPS is roughly 5-10% below consensus, and the shares head towards the DKK 740-840 trough band.

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement. Standards are improving in integration, cash and cost control. They are mixed in commercial execution and in the quality of reported earnings.

Why (improving):

  • Britvic integration. About 30% of synergies were delivered in 2025 against a plan of 10-15%; about 50% more is coming in 2026, for about 80% cumulative by end-2026. The target was raised from GBP 100m to GBP 110m.
  • Cost and profit:
  • Operating costs fell about 1% organically in H1 2026.
  • H1 organic operating profit grew 5.9% against consensus of 4.6%.
  • Free operating cash flow was DKK 3.7bn in H1, against DKK 2.9bn a year earlier.
  • Leverage fell from 3.9x to 3.0x in twelve months.
  • Projects and tools. The Kazakhstan plant opened on schedule. Digital tools and an IT centre in Gurugram, India, support execution:
  • Servd, for customer ordering;
  • One Metric, for shelf execution;
  • OnePlan, for demand planning.
  • Governance:
  • 6 of the 8 shareholder-elected directors are independent.
  • The audit committee is fully independent.
  • The audit is being put out to tender.

Why (against):

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

C+4010th of 35

Judgement. The negatives cluster around four issues: earnings quality, below-the-line leakage, the 2027 volume and cost set-up, and a cost culture being asked to grow.

  • Leakage below operating profit:
  • the hybrid coupon of about DKK 475m a year after tax (Morgan Stanley puts FY2027 EPS dilution at about 2%, Bernstein at about 1%);
  • the Sapporo minority of about DKK 150m of net income a year (our arithmetic);
  • associates moved below operating profit under IFRS 18.

  • the UK deposit return scheme starts in October 2027;
  • Vietnam's excise rises.

  • Return on invested capital fell to about 10-11%.
  • Trade working capital weakened to -15.6% of revenue, from -20.1% excluding Britvic.
  • Supplier finance rose 20% to DKK 3,171m, with payment terms to 180 days. This is a source of cash that could reverse.
  • Culture. The cultural pivot to "calculated risk-taking", with revenue-weighted bonuses, risks diluting the cost discipline the thesis relies on. Watch two signals: marketing at 8.3% of revenue and special items not narrowing.

C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

D4525th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement. In transition and only partly congruent. The goals (growth), the record (cost discipline), the incentives (rewired to revenue) and the value chain (margin first) are not yet one system. Carlsberg is not yet a Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair: companies where goals, strategy, culture, execution and decisions reinforce each other.

Why:

  • For congruence:
  • an engagement score of 82 out of 100, 4 points above the global top quartile;
  • Forbes best employer four years running;
  • the "Funding our Journey" efficiency mindset kept as a permanent operating culture;
  • five growth-culture principles written by the top 90 leaders;
  • the short-term bonus cap raised to 150% of salary, with 10% tied to culture.
  • Against:
  • employee turnover of 16%, higher than premium peers;
  • SpeakUp whistleblower reports up 46% to 334 (38 dismissals);
  • expert reports of decentralised inertia and regional autonomy diluting cost discipline;
  • a deliberate move away from the old "Triple-A" accountability culture.
  • Risk. The culture shift is the mechanism by which the cost discipline the thesis relies on could erode. Watch the marketing ratio and special items.
C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

D4216th of 35

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement. Six risks of that shape, in order of expected cost:

Risk Plausibility Size
1. Structural break in China: austerity plus a channel shift plus aggressive local pricing on Chongqing's mainstream Low to medium within 18 months On our inference China is about a fifth of group operating profit; a 20% fall in China profit is about -4% of group EBIT, and it would also slow deleveraging and the buyback
2. Energy and packaging shock: oil already at USD 111; Jefferies' USD 145 stress raises cost of goods sold 25%; rolling hedges overwhelmed in 2027 Low to medium A 1-2 point margin hit if prices cannot follow quickly
3. Political seizure or war: Russia's 2023 seizure cost DKK 47.7bn; exposures in Ukraine (about 3% of revenue), Kazakhstan and Laos (currency cannot be hedged) Low A few % of EBIT
4. Licence or franchise shock: PepsiCo restructures its system (activist pressure to refranchise), or a licence is lost Very low near term (ten-year rolling agreements) Precedents: San Miguel cost 2-3% of EBIT; Royal Unibrew loses mid-20s% of EBIT
5. Regulatory shock: an EU ban on alcohol advertising, or faster Vietnamese excise Low Tens of basis points of margin and volume in affected markets
6. Operational, product safety or cyber shock: a bottle defect led to a Danish fine in 2019 Low Hard to size

Credit is not a risk of this shape, given the hybrid's equity credit and the BBB+ rating.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

B588th of 35

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The differences between the forecasts and the FY2019-25 record are concentrated in a small number of drivers, and the concentration is of a helpful kind for the next 18 months and a less helpful kind beyond. The change in the profile (EPS compounding at about 8% a year against 3% a year over FY2019-25 on the Viewforth definition, EBIT margin rising about 140bp against flat, free cash flow roughly doubling) comes from four things: the end of three historical drags (Russia, the cost of financing Britvic, special items), the Britvic synergies, the balance-sheet leg (interest, then share count), and price running ahead of cost. The first three are mechanical, dated and largely within management's control, which makes them concentrated and predictable. The fourth is diffuse across 17 PepsiCo markets and 25 beer leadership positions, and it is the one that decides FY2028-30. For the 18-month window, three drivers and one date decide whether the forecasts are met: 2027 price against cost, China's 2027 print against 2026's floods, the Kazakhstan margin step, and the day the buyback is announced. That is a concentrated case.

The decomposition, FY2025 to FY2028 (EPS DKK 60.81 to our DKK 76.4, +DKK 15.6):

Driver Contribution (DKK a share, approximate) Share of the change Nature
Britvic synergies (FY2026-27) +4.0 26% Dated, management-controlled, about 80% delivered by end-2026
Price and mix over cost, and underlying growth, net of the marketing step-up +7.0 45% Diffuse: 17 soft-drink and about 125 beer markets; decided by 2027 cost pass-through
Kazakhstan in-housing and Azerbaijan +2.5 16% Dated, plant open, one region
China (down in 2026, up in 2027) about 0 0% Mechanical round trip; halved at EPS by the 40% minority in Chongqing
Interest saved +4.5 29% Mechanical, mostly already visible
Hybrid coupon -3.6 -23% Fixed to 2031 and 2034
Minorities (Sapporo and Asian recovery) -1.4 -9% Rising, under-modelled by the sell-side
Buyback (from H2 2027) +2.6 17% One decision, one date

The three largest positive drivers (price over cost, interest, synergies) supply about 100% of the net change; the hybrid and minorities take back a third of it. Measured by what is uncertain rather than by what is large, the ranking inverts: interest and synergies are nearly settled; price over cost and the buyback date are open.

Against the last four to six years. Over FY2019-25 the drivers of change were few and external: the Russian seizure (DKK 47.7bn), Britvic's DKK 29.5bn of debt (net interest from DKK 0.75bn to DKK 2.64bn), the 2022-23 cost spike (gross margin from 46.7% to 42.3% on FactSet's basis), and China's weather and consumer cycle. Volume and underlying price did little: organic revenue compounded at about 3% and the company-basis EBIT margin went nowhere (about 15.3% to 15.4%). The forecasts do not assume those external drivers reverse; they assume they stop. That is the most important thing the Consumer can say about concentration: the next four years' difference from the last six is the absence of shocks plus named self-help, not a change in the underlying engine, whose organic revenue rate (about 3%) is forecast to be the same as its history.

How wide each driver's historical range is, and which decided whether past forecasts were met:

  • Organic EBIT against initial guidance. Outcomes have sat above the initial range in nearly every year since 2016; the dispersion is one-sided. Delivery has not been the problem; the beat has simply shrunk (about 300bp in 2024, 200bp in 2025, probably 150-200bp in 2026).
  • Out-year consensus EPS. FactSet's FY2027 consensus has ranged from DKK 79.81 to DKK 69.30 over its life and FY2028's from DKK 91.19 to DKK 75.88, falls of 13-17% from peak to today, mostly before the last twelve months (12-month changes: -0.4% and -4.4%). Part of that was the Britvic financing and the IFRS 18 restatement being modelled in; part was revenue. The lesson is that at Carlsberg the out-year consensus is cut as the year approaches while the in-year number is met. The drivers that did the cutting were below EBIT (interest) and on revenue, not on the EBIT margin.
  • Volume against consensus. Missed in Q1 (price and mix 0.8% against 1.4%), Q2 (+0.8% against +1.2%), H1 (+1.7% against +1.9%) and Q3 2026 (-0.4% against +0.6%), while revenue beat in Q1 and Q3. Volume is the least predictable line; it is also the least important for EBIT, because the company manages its operating leverage (second set, P-01).
  • China. Chongqing Brewery's volume fell 5.9% in Q2 and 9.6% in Q3 2026; the whole sector fell (Tsingtao -9.1%, Budweiser APAC -16.8%). Chinese brewers' volumes rebounded 5-10% in Q1 2023 after the pandemic restrictions lifted, and China Resources Beer recovered within a quarter after the 2016 Yangtze floods. The precedent for a weather year is a full rebound; the precedent for a demand year (2013 onwards, -1.5% a year for a decade) is none. 2026 is some of each: the Carlsberg brand +20% and Heineken +mid-twenties say demand for international premium exists; local brands -11% and on-trade at 47.5% of the market (from 51.5% in 2019) say the channel is structural.
  • Cost-spike years for European brewers. Heineken's organic operating profit growth was cut from an expected 10% to 6.6% within 18 months of the 2022-23 spike; Royal Unibrew's gross margin fell 7.2 points in 2022 on thin hedges; Carlsberg's organic EBIT grew about 5% in 2023 on a 100bp gross-margin fall. Carlsberg's hedging (70% of barley and aluminium by Q3 of the prior year, 90% at the start of the year) and its record put it at the narrow end of the sector's dispersion.

Below-the-line concentration. The FY2027 sell-side EPS spread (DKK 65.57 to 75.79, 15% wide) on revenue within 4% proves that the dispersion of outcomes is now concentrated below EBIT: how each broker treats the hybrid coupon (DKK 475m after tax, about DKK 3.6 a share), minorities (DKK 1,004-1,293m, about DKK 2.2 a share between the extremes), and the buyback (nil to DKK 6-10bn in 2027). These are not forecasting uncertainties about the business; they are modelling choices that the FY2026 results on 9 February 2027, the first full year presented under IFRS 18, will force into line. That is a concentrated, dated event.

Implication for the Consumer. Build the FY2027 cone from four inputs only (price and mix in 2027; China's volume in Q2-Q3 2027; the Kazakhstan step; the buyback date), and treat everything else as noise within ±1% of EPS. For FY2028-30, the single question is whether price over cost can supply 1-2 points of EBIT growth a year without a programme; model it as the diffuse driver it is, with a wide range, and do not let the precision of the near-term build leak into the outer years.

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

Torsten Steenholt returned to run supply chain in November 2025. New heads were named for Western Europe and for CEEI, and a new chief executive for the UK.
  • The board added four directors from 2025, including Bob Kunze-Concewitz, the former chief executive of Campari.

Why:

  • Risks:
  • The chief executive had no consumer-goods experience before 2023.
  • The senior team is small.
  • No successor to the finance director is visible.
  • Regional turnover is high.
  • Peers. Heineken is in transition: its first external chief executive, Rafael Oliveira, starts 1 October 2026. AB InBev is stable. In relative terms, Carlsberg's leadership is the more settled.
C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

B688th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Strategy changed materially in 2024-25. Accelerate SAIL turned Carlsberg into a multi-beverage company and PepsiCo's anchor bottler, with the Sapporo partnership and an option to list India. The change is articulated in numbers and resourced:

  • Britvic for DKK 29.5bn;
  • capital expenditure of DKK 6-7bn a year;
  • a marketing target of about 8.5-9% of revenue.

In the last six months the strategy has not changed; it has been executed and extended:

  • the Sapporo joint venture (July 2026);
  • the Azerbaijan licence;
  • the Georgia and Armenia bottler acquisitions (September 2026);
  • the Kazakhstan plant (October 2026).

The gap is between the revenue algorithm the strategy articulates (4-6%) and delivery (2.7-3.0%).

Why:

  • Logic. One route to market serves beer and soft drinks: one truck, one warehouse, one retail conversation. The Nordic model has run margins above the group average for 25 years, and the playbook is being replicated in the UK, Central Asia and the Caucasus.
  • Choices:
  • let low-margin mainstream volume go;
  • price in line with local inflation;
  • grow premium, alcohol-free and soft drinks;
  • "drive value, not empty volume" (chief executive, Q3 2026).
  • Resourcing gaps:
  • Sales and marketing were 8.3% of revenue against the group's own 9% framework.
  • An expert view is that matching Heineken's premium brand equity would need marketing to double.
  • Leverage constrains capital until 2027.
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

C+609th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Guidance was raised modestly in 2026:

  • FY2026 organic operating profit guidance was narrowed upward from 2-6% to 4-6% (19 August) and repeated on 29 October.
  • Net finance cost guidance was cut from DKK 2.2bn to DKK 1.8bn.
  • Expected translation moved from -DKK 100m to flat to +DKK 100m.

The mid-term ambition was raised in February 2024, from 3-5% to 4-6% organic revenue growth, with operating profit to grow faster than revenue. The October 2025 capital markets day reaffirmed it and set two further targets: gross margin back to 47-49% pro forma, and leverage below 2.5x by end-2027. There has been no new mid-term raise since. The important nuance: on the restated base, FY2026 consensus sits at the guidance midpoint, so the narrowing created no room for upgrades.

Why:

  • The revenue target. It has not been met in any year since it was set. The organic revenue part of the 2023-25 incentive plan paid 12.10 out of 20.00 against a 4% target.
  • Informal ambition above formal guidance:
  • At the FY2025 results, management said the business "should be delivering 4% to 6% revenue growth" with "higher EBIT growth than 4% to 6%".
  • At the capital markets day, it spoke of "compounding high-single-digit operating profit growth over the next three years".

This is the ceiling management is signalling for FY2027-28 (see C-85).

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

B589th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The perimeter has been changed more than reduced. Reductions are limited and opportunistic, and large additions outweigh them. The standout is Sapporo: Carlsberg sold 25% of its Southeast Asia and Hong Kong business for USD 643m, at 21.3x 2025 EBIT and 17.0x EBITDA, about double the group's own multiple. It kept control, gained perpetual brand rights and took about 0.2x off leverage. That is disciplined monetisation, not retreat.

Why:

  • Reductions:
  • Russia: forced, deconsolidated in 2023 and sold in 2024.
  • Teisseire: local production ends and sales are outsourced (167 net job losses).
  • Low-margin private-label soft-drink contracts in France were pruned.
  • Norway's distribution was restructured.
  • The UK San Miguel licence ended, though Carlsberg did not choose that.
  • Britvic Brazil is under strategic review, a possible disposal deferred while the market is weak.
  • Additions:
  • Britvic;
  • PepsiCo in Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan and Azerbaijan;
  • the Georgian and Armenian bottlers (about 0.2% of sales);
  • PepsiCo in Denmark, Finland and the Baltics from 2029.
  • Implication for the model. Sapporo leaves EBITDA consolidated, but from closing the minority leaks about DKK 150m of net income a year (our arithmetic). Against that, interest saved on about DKK 4.2bn of proceeds is roughly DKK 150m before tax, so the deal is about neutral to EPS and positive for leverage.
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

C6013th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Most of the entries are new categories in existing countries, not new countries. That is sensible: they are close adjacencies that reuse existing routes to market, customers and breweries. In the Wisdom Library's "Core versus Adjacency Growth" terms they share channels with the core, so their failure rate should be well below the roughly three-in-four for distant moves. They are small in group terms and back-loaded.

Why:

  • Kazakhstan. The PepsiCo licence doubles the local business, making it a top-ten market. A USD 344m plant opened on 1 October 2026, with capacity of 1 billion litres.
  • Azerbaijan (1 January 2027) doubles a business with sales of about DKK 150m, adding about 0.3% to group volume. Georgia and Armenia add about 0.2% to group sales.
  • Sapporo Premium went live in the UK and Myanmar on 1 August 2026, with an ambition of ten times the volume by 2035. It fills the premium gap left by San Miguel.
  • India. A new plant in Karnataka (INR 3.5bn) and six local contract brewers.
  • Limits:
  • No exposure to Latin America or Africa.
  • The Uzbekistan PepsiCo licence was lost for lack of a brewery there.
  • A Turkish move is unlikely (Redburn).
  • Jefferies calls the footprint "the narrowest and most concentrated" among global brewers.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

D4525th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms, and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent?

Judgement. The rises are nominal, not demonstrably real:

  • H1 2026. Price and mix rose 1.0%, against 1.4% expected, while inflation ran at about 2-3% in the main markets. In real terms that is roughly flat to negative.
  • Q3 2026. Revenue per hectolitre rose 3%, positive in every region and the best figure of the year. Part of it is geographic mix from losing lower-priced Chinese volume (see C-06).
  • 2027 policy. "Price in line with local CPI" and "cover over 80% of input inflation through revenue management". The aim is to protect gross margin, not to expand real price.

Real pricing is latent and increasingly noted by management, but it is contested in UK and Polish mainstream and in Chinese super-premium.

Why:

  • Signs of latent real pricing:
  • The premium Carlsberg brand grew volume 6% globally and 16% in Western Europe in H1.
  • India's mix of states and brands is improving.
  • Laos took high-single-digit price rises against inflation.
  • Morgan Stanley noted a historical record of pricing above inflation (2021).
  • Signs of erosion:
  • Polish volume was lost to front-loaded price rises.
  • A Swiss retailer kept products off its shelves for 4-6 weeks in Q4 2025.
  • The UK mainstream brand lost on-trade share.
  • 1664 Blanc fell 1% in H1 and 5% in Q3, a sign that super-premium has reached its price limit in China.
  • 2027 is the test. About 160bp of cost headwind requires about 1-1.5% of price on top of normal mix. Price and mix below 1% while input inflation runs above 5% would be the warning.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

F4730th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. M&A stepped up sharply once, with Britvic, completed in January 2025:

  • equity of DKK 29.5bn and enterprise value of about GBP 4.1bn;
  • 13.6x EBITDA, which DNB Carnegie called "highly expensive";
  • leverage up from about 1.7x to 3.28x.

Strategically it is coherent. Carlsberg became the UK's second-largest beverage supplier and PepsiCo's anchor bottler in Europe, using the Nordic model. Integration is ahead of plan. Since then M&A has been small bolt-ons (Georgia, Armenia), alongside earlier tidying of ownership:

  • the purchase of the Indian minority partner's stake for USD 744m in November 2024;
  • full consolidation of Nepal.

The disciplined part is the speed of synergies and the Sapporo sale. The undisciplined part is the price paid and the fall in returns: return on invested capital fell from 13.8% (2024) to 10.1% (2025) and was 10.8% on a rolling basis in mid-2026.

Why:

  • Integration evidence:
  • Synergies are ahead of schedule (C-14).
  • All people changes in the UK and Ireland were concluded faster than planned.
  • The Pepsi portfolio gained more than 1 point of UK volume share in Q1 2026.
  • Risks:
  • Two-thirds of the UK leadership came from Britvic, and GBP 16.5m of retention payments were made.
  • Departures have been reported (Redburn).
  • The deal adds a lower-margin franchise model (soft drinks sell at about a 20% discount to the group's average price per hectolitre, a drag of about 30bp a year on mix).
  • Outlook. No large deal is likely before leverage falls below 2.5x.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

C+589th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. The balance sheet was used heavily for Britvic, and buybacks have been suspended since July 2024. The next 6-18 months are about reversing that, and management increasingly talks of buybacks resuming once leverage is below 2.5x:

  • "to comfortably resume share buybacks";
  • brokers date the restart anywhere from the FY2026 results (February 2027, Jefferies) to H2 2027 (DNB Carnegie).

The dividend is progressive: DKK 29.00 for FY2025, up 7%, a payout of about 48% of adjusted profit.

Our arithmetic on leverage (company basis):

  • About 2.4-2.6x at end-2026 if Sapporo closes on time (Jefferies has 2.46x); about 2.7x if it does not.
  • India is not needed to reach the target, but would bring it forward by about 0.3x.
  • For the model: a buyback announcement is roughly even odds in February 2027 and likely by the H1 2027 results (August 2027).

Why:

  • Capacity once restarted. About DKK 3-4bn a year, the pace of 2021-23 (DKK 3.2-4.4bn), or about 2.5-3.3% of shares a year at today's market value of about DKK 124bn. Jefferies' DKK 6-10bn for FY2027 looks aggressive while the hybrid coupon, minority dividends and capital expenditure of DKK 6-7bn absorb cash.
  • Not in consensus. The share count stays flat at about 132m, so a restart adds about 1-3% to FY2028 EPS above consensus.
  • The hybrid. The EUR 1.8bn hybrid of May 2026 bought rating headroom (50% equity credit) at a coupon of about DKK 475m a year after tax.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–64

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Seven positive forces matter, in descending order of weight for the model:

  1. Kazakhstan in-housing. It ends the toll-filling margin drag, which took 40bp off the CEEI margin in H1 2026, and lifts 2027 profit. Bernstein models CEEI operating profit at DKK 3,451m (2025), DKK 3,674m (2026) and DKK 4,229m (2027), a CEEI margin of 20.5% in 2027 and 21.2% in 2028. That 2027 step of about DKK 555m for the region (India growth plus Kazakhstan's recovery) would be roughly 65% of consensus group EBIT growth in FY2027 (DKK 852m). FY2027 consensus therefore leans heavily on CEEI, and the Consumer should model Kazakhstan's margin step and India's growth explicitly.
  2. Buyback restart (C-19).
  3. PepsiCo's repeated choice of Carlsberg. Each licence lowers the cost of serving the next. DNB Carnegie puts each mid-sized territory at +4-8% to EPS over time; we treat that as aggressive.
  4. India listing option. About USD 700m for a 25-40% float, net proceeds of DKK 5-6bn, about 0.3x off leverage and 1.7-2.0% accretive to EPS (Bernstein). Its timing is uncertain.
  5. Sapporo Premium brand rights in the UK, Myanmar and, in perpetuity, Southeast Asia.
  6. Currency now a tailwind (+DKK 100m in 2026).
  7. Leadership alignment. Insider buying, an engagement score of 82 out of 100, and the return of the supply-chain chief.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

F4827th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. They have not. The Carlsberg Foundation holds about 30% of the capital and 77.5-78% of the votes, and its charter requires it to keep 51%. It has indicated continuity, not change. Its public statement of 17 August 2026 ("Let's safeguard one of Danish business's greatest strengths") defends foundation ownership. It receives about DKK 1.1bn of dividends a year, and historically it took part pro rata in buybacks (selling B shares worth DKK 595m in 2024). So it is aligned with a progressive dividend and a buyback restart, and not with a sale of the company or a radical strategic shift.

Why:

  • No external catalyst. No activist can act, and the shares carry no takeover premium.
  • Insulation. Management is insulated, which helps long-term execution. But it delays correction if execution fails: the gap between voting control and economic ownership is 47.5 points, and independence across the whole board is only 46%.
  • Charter change. The Foundation may now fall below 25% of capital while keeping 51% of votes, which allows it to be diluted without losing control.
  • Implication. Strategy changes will come from management alone. The model should not price any corporate-event optionality beyond India and buybacks.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

C+689th of 35

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement. Strong, broad and well resourced. Nine of the twelve internal change forces assessed in the memo are under way, at least five of them strongly:

  • chief executive;
  • strategy;
  • M&A
  • standards;
  • new products.

All are resourced with capital and people. The six-month change is execution of change already announced, not new change, and most of its profit effect is already in consensus (synergies, lower finance costs, Kazakhstan margin recovery). Three unpriced positives remain:

  • the buyback restart;
  • any beat on 2026 guidance (about +1% to FY2026 EBIT if delivery lands at 6%);
  • Kazakhstan beating consensus.

The one unproven change is revenue growth.

Implication for the model. Credit the changes with:

  • 5-7% organic operating profit growth;
  • about 30bp a year of operating margin;
  • deleveraging to about 2.5x by end-2026 or mid-2027.

Do not credit them with the 4-6% revenue algorithm. Use about 3.5-4%.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

C6013th of 35

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

C6013th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The structure is a concentrated, rational oligopoly that supports price discipline: low private label, pass-through of excise, and a margin-first stance at group level. Carlsberg ranks third globally but leads locally in 25 markets carrying about 70% of its volume. The value chain is pressing on volume:

  • consolidating retailers;
  • competitors cutting price for share in UK and Polish mainstream;
  • Heineken out-executing in Chinese and Vietnamese premium;
  • regulators adding excise, sugar and packaging costs.

Consolidation in beer favours AB InBev and Heineken, not Carlsberg. Carlsberg is instead winning a different consolidation, PepsiCo bottling and multi-beverage routes to market, which brings volume at lower revenue per hectolitre. Precedent says brewers outperform through self-help, deleveraging and buybacks when volume stabilises, and underperform when the premium engine stalls. Carlsberg fits the first on the balance sheet but not the second on volume.

For the model: pricing linked to local inflation is supported by structure, mainstream share should drift down, and geographic and category mix should depress revenue per hectolitre while the cost of serving falls.

3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers
C-3242C-3352C-3435C-3736C-3845C-3952
C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?F4233rd of 35full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

D5222nd of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. On balance helpful in Europe, through the multi-beverage offer. A hindrance where grocery is concentrated. Fragile in Chinese distribution.

Why:

  • Helping:
  • One retail conversation, joint business plans and single-truck deliveries ("no competitor discusses beverage portfolios in totality", Jefferies).
  • Soft-drink scale opened Amazon to Carlsberg's beer.
  • DraughtMaster equipment keeps beer fresh for 30 days, against 2-3 days in steel kegs.
  • On-trade loans in five countries lock in customers.
  • In growth markets, 95% of volume goes through traditional trade, where Carlsberg has more leverage.
  • Hindering:
  • European buying alliances force price rollbacks and delistings (the Swiss dispute in Q4 2025).
  • Chinese distributors were left with elevated stock after the floods and destocked, a typical 2-3 month lag.
  • India has 450 dealers under state-regulated pricing.
  • Vietnam's distributor network is being rebuilt.
  • In digital ordering, Carlsberg lags BEES and eazle, which own the relationship with small outlets.
  • For the model. Destocking in China can make reported volume lag consumer demand by a quarter. Q4 2026 may still be soft even if consumers stabilise.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

F3529th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Competitors' behaviour hurts. Heineken is the main threat:

  • China, through China Resources Beer;
  • Vietnam, where its volume is up in the low twenties;
  • the UK, with the Foster's alcohol-strength price cut;
  • its global premium platform;
  • India, through United Breweries.

The others:

  • AB InBev: Chinese off-trade investment and more than USD 7bn a year of marketing.
  • Asahi: Polish discounting.
  • China Resources Beer and Tsingtao: China.
  • The Coca-Cola system (Coca-Cola Europacific Partners and Coca-Cola HBC): in soft drinks, Coke's dominant share could force promotional pricing in the UK (Deutsche Bank).

Carlsberg's position is robust in its strongholds and fragile in mainstream and super-premium.

Why:

  • Relative momentum. Q3 organic revenue: Heineken +4.6% (volume +3.9%), AB InBev +4.9%, Carlsberg +3.0%. Carlsberg ranks around the 40th percentile of its peers on momentum.
  • Offsets. Vietnam's price war has eased, and the joint Carlsberg-Britvic offer is winning UK contracts.

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Actor How the disruption acts Effect on Carlsberg

Industry structure

10 answers
Judgement60C-2466C-2582C-2655C-2750C-2865C-2963C-3168C-3578C-3645
Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)C6013th of 35full answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

C-6614th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Where does the company rank by market share or size, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

Judgement. Carlsberg is third of the three global brewers. It has about 5% of global beer volume, against about 25% for AB InBev and about 12% for Heineken, and about 3% of global beer EBIT, against about 36% for AB InBev. Globally the gap to the top two is unbridgeable. Locally it is number one or two in 25 beer markets carrying about 70% of its volume:

  • the Nordics, Switzerland and the Baltics;
  • Western China (Chongqing, Xinjiang);
  • Laos and Central Vietnam;
  • Kazakhstan and Ukraine;
  • Malaysia and Singapore;
  • India, where it is second.

But weighted by volume, it leads in only 24% of its volume and is a challenger in 66%. AB InBev leads in 75% and Heineken in 52%. The second statement is the one that bites on pricing power. In the UK it is now the second-largest combined beer and soft-drinks supplier.

Why:

  • Profit sits where it leads. Asia earns a 26.3% operating margin and a return on invested capital of 81.8% excluding goodwill. Western Europe, where it is mostly a challenger, earns 13.8%.
  • India gains. Share rose from 5% (2011) to 17-23% in its active states (2025), with 40-45% in Maharashtra. United Breweries (Heineken) still holds about 50% of India.
  • Implication. Regional margins are structural to position. Do not model convergence of Western Europe towards Asia.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

B+824th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Highly concentrated, both globally and in most local markets:

  • Top five. More than 50% of global volume (53%) and 56% of value, more than double the roughly 25% of 2000.
  • Top three. 43% of revenue and 51% of global beer EBIT.

By volume the curve is steep, but not five-to-one. The top ten held about 1.34bn of 1.86bn hectolitres (about 72%), so brewers six to ten hold roughly 19%, against 53% for the top five, a ratio of about 3:1. By profit it is much steeper: AB InBev alone takes 36% of EBIT. Locally, about 60% of beer is drunk in markets of high or medium concentration. Latin America runs as duopolies and near-monopolies, while Western Europe is more fragmented, with lower margins despite high prices per unit.

Why it matters for the model:

  • Concentration underpins price discipline:
  • private label is only about 9% of beverages;
  • excise is passed through within months;
  • beer demand is inelastic (price elasticity -0.30 to -0.47).
  • This is what makes inflation-linked pricing assumptions credible.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

D5520th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Shares are stable in Carlsberg's strongholds over three to five years and eroding in mainstream lager (the UK, Poland and China's provinces) and in Chinese super-premium. Smaller local players have taken share from multinationals in Western Europe since 2022 (Deutsche Bank). New entrants have taken negligible share in beer, where barriers to entry are high. They have taken meaningful share through substitutes:

  • private label in European grocery;
  • non-beer drinks in China, such as milk tea;
  • spirits-based ready-to-drink cocktails in the US.

Why:

  • Where Carlsberg gained:
  • India, from 5% to 17-23% in active states over 2011-25;
  • UK soft drinks, with the Pepsi portfolio up more than 1 point of volume share in Q1 2026 and value growth ahead of the market (Bernstein);
  • Chongqing Brewery, up 30bp in a Chinese market that fell 4% in 2025.
  • Where it lost:
  • UK on-trade mainstream;
  • Poland, with volume down by low double digits;
  • China premium against Heineken.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

D5017th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Share and profit consolidate towards the largest and, in 2025-26, towards the most advantaged in premium beer. Carlsberg is not among the main winners in beer. It is a winner of a different consolidation: PepsiCo bottling and multi-beverage routes to market in Northern Europe and Central Asia.

Why:

  • Towards the largest. AB InBev earns an EBITDA margin of about 36% against about 21% for Carlsberg and Heineken: the prize for scale and cost suppression.
  • Towards the most advantaged.
  • Heineken's partnership with China Resources Beer took its licensed volume in China up close to 30% in H1 2026.
  • Heineken Silver grew about 30% in 2025.
  • In Q3, Heineken's Asia Pacific volume rose 17.3% while Carlsberg's China volume fell about 10%.
  • Where Carlsberg wins. PepsiCo keeps choosing the incumbent bottler with a dense route to market. The footprint goes from 5 markets in 2024 to 17 by 2029, taking about 13% of Royal Unibrew's sales from 2029.
  • Implication. Expect Carlsberg's premium beer share in Asia to drift down while its soft-drink and Central Asian share rises. The mix effect is negative for revenue per hectolitre and neutral to positive for return on capital at the margin, once plants are in-house.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C-6517th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. At group level, all three majors put margin and return first. Locally, they turn to volume when a rule change opens a window:

  • the UK's duty by alcohol strength;
  • Poland's deposit return scheme;
  • the 2025 Vietnamese price war.

Some will accept local losses (Cambodia, with local overcapacity). Industry prices do not generally fall when volume falls: list prices and excise pass-through hold. But promotional intensity rises in weak retail: 31.5% of European low- and no-alcohol volume is sold on promotion, up 2.3 points, and Southern European supermarkets run promotions on up to 70% of volume.

Why:

  • Carlsberg's own stance. It concedes mainstream volume rather than fight ("we are here to drive value for our shareholders and not drive empty volume"). Its target is one-third volume and two-thirds price and mix in revenue growth.
  • Peers.
  • Heineken prices "slightly below CPI" to win back off-trade volume.
  • Heineken's price and mix slowed to 0.7-1.8% in Q3 as it led on volume.
  • AB InBev reinvests in marketing, taking 34bp off its EBITDA margin in Q3.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

C+6312th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Reasonably stable. The changes over 18-36 months were driven by rules and events, not by temperament:

  • the UK alcohol-strength price cuts came from a duty rule;
  • Poland's discounting came with the deposit return scheme;
  • Vietnam's 2025 price war has passed.

That makes them predictable but durable. No shark attack on Carlsberg has been observed: Asahi in Poland is the closest case, and Carlsberg let volume go rather than fight. The new Heineken chief executive, Rafael Oliveira (from 1 October 2026), brings Kraft Heinz discipline and a mandate to close a 180-200bp margin gap. That points to cost-led margin repair rather than price aggression, though marketing intensity is rising.

Where the risk sits. Western China is the place a shark attack would hurt. Carlsberg's weakness there could invite China Resources Beer, or Heineken through it, to press mainstream pricing in Chongqing, which Carlsberg's China profit depends on. This is low probability but high impact. Watch Chongqing Brewery's half-year disclosures; it is a listed subsidiary.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

B689th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. Supportive for price and profit, fragile for volume in some local markets. The structure supports price and profit through:

  • an oligopoly;
  • high barriers to entry: capital, brands, locked-up draught taps, advertising bans and excise regimes;
  • pass-through of excise and cost;
  • low private label.

The support is real, but not to be leaned on in every market: in the last twelve months, competitors cut price for share in two of Carlsberg's important markets.

For the model. Pricing linked to local inflation (2-3% a year) is supported. Mainstream volume share should be modelled flat to down. The upside from structure is that 2027 cost inflation will probably be priced through across the industry. The downside is that a weak Carlsberg region invites local price aggression.

C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?A781st of 35full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

D4525th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement. A real threat, growing slowly. Carlsberg owns part of the substitute set: soft drinks are about 30% of volume, plus alcohol-free beer. The threat is shifting from brand-against-brand to share of what people drink.

Why:

  • The substitutes:
  • abstention and moderation (22% of consumers abstained over a six-month period, per DNB Carnegie);
  • spirits and ready-to-drink cocktails;
  • cannabis drinks in the US;
  • weight-loss (GLP-1) drugs, which cut drinking among middle-aged users (Morgan Stanley).
  • Ownership. Carlsberg owns the fastest-growing substitutes in its markets (alcohol-free +11%, soft drinks +9%). The cost is mix: soft drinks sell at about a 20% lower price per hectolitre, a drag of about 30bp a year on margin (Redburn).
  • Implication. Model mainstream beer volume at -1% to -3% a year in Western Europe, offset by soft drinks at +3-5%, as in DNB Carnegie's -2% beer and +3% soft-drink compound rates.

Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

Memo topic 3, Value chain, structure, model, financials, management: the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

C-5616th of 358 answers
Judgement56C-4157C-4252C-4344C-4455C-4542C-4652C-4752
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

C-5616th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Carlsberg's advantage is local density and positions, not global scale or premium brand leadership. Its moats are real in 25 leadership markets and strengthening in multi-beverage distribution, where PepsiCo's repeated choice of Carlsberg and single-truck economics compound mechanically. In premium beer it is losing ground to Heineken in Asia, and in digital ordering it lags AB InBev. Pricing power exists in nominal terms (cost and excise pass-through) but has not been shown in real terms recently. Customer utility is not inferior in strongholds and is inferior in Chinese and Vietnamese premium.

The total addressable market supports 3-4% organic revenue growth, below the 4-6% target. For the Consumer: model pricing linked to local inflation with lags, mainstream share drifting down, premium growing low single digits outside China, soft drinks taking group mix down by about 30bp a year, and no mechanical margin drift beyond named programmes.
