C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

D5216th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Carlsberg is mid-scale.

Company Revenue Employees
AB InBev USD 59.3bn about 126,000
Heineken EUR 28.9bn about 87,000
Carlsberg DKK 89.1bn (about USD 13bn) about 37,000
Royal Unibrew DKK 15.7bn

Carlsberg sells 148m hectolitres of all beverages. Its scale confers:

  • local density and procurement leverage;
  • relevance to retailers in the UK and the Nordics;
  • PepsiCo's confidence as anchor bottler.

It does not confer global marketing parity: AB InBev spends more than USD 7bn a year on sales and marketing. Nor does it give the cost of goods sold of the leader.

Why:

  • The cost gap is the measurable scale penalty. On consensus, AB InBev's EBITDA margin is about 36% against Carlsberg's 21%.
  • Carlsberg's answer is density, not size. Combining beer and soft drinks lowers the cost of serving small accounts. That is the mechanism behind margins in the Nordics running above the group average, and the reason the Britvic logic is sound.
C-43How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?F4433rd of 35full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

D5520th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. Not inferior in its strongholds:

  • local mainstream leaders;
  • the premium Carlsberg brand (+6% globally, +16% in Western Europe, and international premium brands +20% in China in H1);
  • UK soft drinks, where it gained share;
  • India, where it gained share.

Retailers value the combined offer, with multi-year joint business plans. But the 2025-26 evidence shows it is inferior to Heineken's premium platform in China and Vietnam, and losing to cheaper rivals in UK mainstream on-trade. At this stage inferiority in premium beer cannot be disproved.

Why: In the Wisdom Library's "Share of the Consumer Mind" terms, mainstream lager is bought by indifferent beer drinkers (a weak habit, easily switched on price). The real habit sits in local power brands and draught lock-in. The multi-beverage offer's utility is to the retailer and outlet (fewer suppliers, less administration), not to the consumer. That makes it durable in the customer relationship but no protection against consumers trading down.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

C-4217th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility?

Judgement. Pricing power is demonstrable but nominal: Carlsberg can pass through costs and excise with a lag. It has not shown real price growth of 2% or more a year recently. In 2026 real price was roughly flat in H1 (price and mix +1.0%) and better in Q3 (+3% per hectolitre, partly from mix). Erosion is visible in Poland, the UK, China super-premium and the Swiss retailer dispute.

Why:

  • Evidence of use:
  • excise passed on "immediately or within a few months";
  • 2022-23 inflation recovered with a lag;
  • high-single-digit price rises in Laos;
  • "when we see cost inflation coming through the base, we will cover that with price" (chief executive, Q3 2026).
  • Evidence of limits:
  • Polish volume lost to front-loaded pricing;
  • a 4-6 week delisting in Switzerland;
  • Carlsberg Danish Pilsner's share loss in the UK;
  • 1664 Blanc's price limit in China.
  • 2027 test. About 1-1.5% of extra price is needed to cover about 160bp of cost headwind. Morgan Stanley's view that pricing is "highly resilient" rests on Q3 alone.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

C+5210th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto?

Judgement. Partly and de facto, not as a stated principle:

  • Synergy and procurement savings fund price restraint (pricing near or slightly below inflation is the industry's affordability habit) and brand investment.
  • The VMx price-pack tools tailor value to the outlet.
  • In soft drinks, PepsiCo's co-funded marketing passes system scale to customers.

The "value over volume" stance and the gross-margin rebuild target mean savings are retained more than shared. That is consistent with a margin-recovery company, but not with the Costco-style flywheel (sharing scale savings with customers to win more volume) that would widen the moat mechanically.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

D5219th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement. The picture is mixed.

Mechanical (improving with each period):

  • Multi-beverage density: each licence lowers the cost of serving the next; Nordic-Baltic bottling consolidates in 2029.
  • PepsiCo keeps choosing the incumbent.
  • Negative working capital.
  • Procurement scale.

Heroic (needing sustained effort):

  • Premiumisation in China, a decade's work: "Big Cities" went from 9 in 2017 to 99 in 2025.
  • Share gains in India.
  • The gross-margin rebuild.

The financials have not compounded mechanically against peers:

  • return on invested capital fell (13.8% to 10.1%);
  • FactSet's gross margin fell from 46.7% (FY2019) to 42.3% (FY2025);
  • Carlsberg's sell-side momentum is D against AB InBev's A.

Implication. The model should not assume margin drift up without named actions. The named actions for 2026-28 (Britvic synergies, Kazakhstan, Teisseire, pricing) are finite.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

D5221st of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement. Moderate, not big. The ambition is:

  • 4-6% organic revenue growth, with operating profit growing faster;
  • "compounding high-single-digit operating profit growth";
  • becoming PepsiCo's anchor bottler in Europe and Central Asia;
  • multiplying Sapporo Premium's volume ten times by 2035;
  • a cultural reset towards growth and "calculated risk-taking" ("all about playing offense and growth", chief executive, October 2025).

Against peers it is similar in scale to Heineken's EverGreen 2030 (mid-single-digit revenue, high-single-digit operating profit) and narrower than AB InBev's. AB InBev targets 4-8% organic EBITDA growth from a 36% margin, with a digital platform (BEES) and a beyond-beer agenda. Carlsberg's ambition is adjacency-led, not category-defining.

Why: Ambition is tempered by the footprint: embedded beer volume growth of 0.3% and the narrowest geographic spread among the majors. The bold act was Britvic. The ambition now is to make it pay.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

C6413th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities?

Judgement. Smart and congruent with the assets; bold in Britvic's size; less dynamic in China. It widens the moat in multi-beverage markets (UK, Nordics, Central Asia, the Caucasus) through shared trucks, warehouses and retail conversations. It treats growth as a priority on paper, and pays for it through incentives. It is resilient across environments through diversification and pricing discipline, but constrained by leverage until 2027.

Why:

  • Right choices:
  • letting mainstream volume go rather than buying it;
  • asset-light monetisation of Asia through Sapporo;
  • bringing Kazakhstan production in-house;
  • preparing a partial listing of India;
  • alcohol-free innovation (50 new alcohol-free beers in 2025).
  • Weak spots:
  • No answer yet to Heineken's premium platform in Asia.
  • In China the response is to cede mainstream and push premium and e-commerce (RedNote, Meituan). That is defensive while 1664 Blanc falls.
  • Digital ordering lags AB InBev's BEES.
  • The revenue target has never been met.
  • The Jefferies counterpoint. "Beyond beer" strategies have a mixed record (US hard seltzer). Soft drinks are a lower-margin franchise business with concentrate economics set by PepsiCo.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

C5513th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company put shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent is its governance?

Judgement. Shareholders come first within the Foundation's frame, and the governance scaffolding is prudent. But the controlling structure caps minority influence.

Evidence for shareholders first:

  • The dividend was raised 7% through the deleveraging, at a payout of about 48% of adjusted profit.
  • Buybacks were paused rather than stretching the balance sheet.
  • DKK 20bn was bought back over 2019-24.
  • Pay is tied to total shareholder return, EPS, organic revenue, return on invested capital and ESG. The relative total-return part vested at zero, so pay responded to performance.
  • The chief executive buys shares on the open market.

Scaffolding:

  • 6 of the 8 shareholder-elected directors are independent;
  • the audit committee is fully independent;
  • the audit is out to tender;
  • clawback and malus clauses;
  • annual director elections.

Limits:

  • The Foundation holds 30% of capital but 77.5-78% of votes, a gap of 47.5 points.
  • Only 46% of the whole board is independent.
  • There is no separate nomination committee.
  • The charter allows the Foundation's capital to fall below 25% while it keeps 51% of votes.
  • On contested matters, minority shareholders come after the Foundation.

Why it is prudent: The Foundation's interests (a progressive dividend and long-term value) align with minority shareholders on dividends and deleveraging. They do not align on a sale or a radical restructuring.

Against:

For the model. Expect a disciplined return to buybacks of DKK 3-4bn a year once leverage is below 2.5x, and no large M&A before then.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

D4223rd of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Adjusted EPS grew about 5.5% a year from 2023 to 2025 (DKK 54.6 to DKK 60.81) on organic revenue growth of about 2-3%. On the Viewforth definition, EPS has been flat at DKK 64-65 since 2022, through the Russia exit, Britvic and higher interest. Consensus: +6.9% (FY2026), +6.5% (FY2027) and +9.8% (FY2028), a compound rate of 8.2% over FY2026-28, on revenue growth of 3.6-3.8% a year. Our cross-check:

  • FY2027 EPS growth roughly equals EBIT growth (about 6%). Lower interest adds about 2 points, but the full-year hybrid coupon takes about 2 points and the Sapporo minority about 1.5 points.
  • FY2028 accelerates to about 10%, as those one-off steps drop out and interest keeps falling.
  • A buyback restarted in mid-2027 adds 1-3% to FY2028.

Profit growth is durable (guidance culture, synergies, pricing). Revenue growth is weak, and earnings quality is muddied.

Why:

  • The two anchors to avoid. Neither the 14% a year cited in one Key Question analysis (built on Bernstein) nor Jefferies' 6.9% is right. The Wisdom Library's "The Vaguest Explanation Wins A Fewest-Contradictions Contest" warns that each analysis crowned its own framing.
  • Central range. About 8-9% a year over FY2026-29, with a downside of about 6% if every item below EBITDA goes against the company (the memo's adverse case).
  • Last three years against the next three. EPS growth accelerates from about 5.5% to about 8-9%, almost all from the profit and below-the-line lines, not from revenue.
C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

The gap includes amortisation of the intangibles booked on buying Britvic, and items previously called special. FactSet's standardised adjusted EBIT (DKK 11,927m) differs from CPM by about 15%.
  • Hybrid. It is classified as equity, so its coupon sits outside finance costs but must be deducted for EPS: about DKK 475m a year after tax, about 5% of net income. Brokers model it unevenly: FY2027 EPS estimates range from DKK 65.57 to DKK 75.00 on revenue estimates within about 4% of each other.
  • Minorities. They rise with the Sapporo joint venture. Chongqing Brewery is a listed subsidiary.
  • Tax. About 23% guided, against 24.7% reported in 2025.
  • Other flags. Revenue recognition is a key audit matter because of complex discount structures. Supplier finance is growing.
  • Our decomposition of consensus (assuming 23% tax): minorities plus hybrid take about DKK 0.9bn in FY2026, rising to about DKK 1.1-1.2bn in FY2027-28.
  • For the model. Build EPS from CPM operating profit:
  • less net finance costs (DKK 1.8bn in 2026, falling about DKK 150-200m a year with debt);
  • plus associates (now below operating profit);
  • less tax at 23-24%;
  • less minorities, including Sapporo;
  • less the hybrid coupon.

Then divide by about 132m shares until buybacks restart, and reconcile to the company's adjusted EPS (DKK 60.81 base).

C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

F4027th of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify EPS and risk, and how comfortable is it?

Judgement. Elevated but falling, and comfortable for an investment-grade issuer:

  • Leverage. 3.0x on the company's basis at H1 2026, heading to about 2.5x by end-2026 or 2027. FactSet's basis gives 3.6x, the highest in the peer table: Heineken 3.0x, AB InBev 2.9x, Diageo 3.1x, Royal Unibrew 2.2x.
  • Net interest. DKK 2.64bn in 2025, guided at DKK 1.8bn for 2026 (excluding currency effects).
  • Refinancing. 18% of gross debt must be refinanced by 2028, against 29% at Heineken and 12% at AB InBev.
  • Rate sensitivity. Refinancing a year of maturities at 100bp higher cuts pre-tax profit by 0.8%.
  • Rating. BBB+ on the Britvic notes (Fitch). The hybrid's 50% equity credit protects it.

Why:

  • Amplification. Each DKK 5bn of debt repaid saves roughly DKK 175m of interest at about 3.5%, about +1.5% to EPS. Deleveraging from about DKK 57bn to about DKK 47bn by 2027 is worth roughly 3 points of EPS. Part of that is offset by the hybrid coupon, which costs about 5% of net income.
  • Comfort. There is no covenant concern. The only tail risk is a sharp fall in EBITDA, which would delay buybacks rather than threaten solvency.
  • For the model. Interest falls with net debt, and the hybrid coupon stays fixed until first call (non-call periods of 5.25 and 8 years). Step-up terms after the call dates are not disclosed.

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

Memo topic 5, Pricing power, leverage, cost control, feedback loops: the Rater's score

5a

Pricing power

3 answers
C-1745C-4542P-0446
C-17To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management?D4525th of 35full answer under 2b Internal corporate change forces
C-45To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?C-4217th of 35full answer under 4a Business quality
P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

C4613th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Price contributes most of Carlsberg's revenue growth over the forecast. Its job, though, is to defend margin, not expand it. We expect pure price of about 2.5% a year (in line with local inflation, plus excise pass-through). Category and geographic mix reduce that to growth in revenue per hectolitre of about 2.0-2.3% in 2027 and 2.3-2.5% a year in 2028-30. With volume of 0 to +0.5%, price and mix supply about 75-85% of organic revenue growth, above the company's own target of two-thirds.

In margin terms:

  • 2027. Price covers roughly 85-95% of input-cost inflation in absolute money, consistent with the stated policy of covering "over 80%". Gross profit grows, but gross margin percentage slips 0-30bp.
  • 2028-30. If input inflation moderates, price runs slightly ahead of cost and supplies about 1-2 points of EBIT growth a year.

Real pricing, meaning price above inflation, is not in our base. The evidence does not show it in 2025-26, and competitors (Heineken at 70-90% of CPI in Europe) and retailers cap it. Price is the single largest swing factor for 2027 EBIT: each 0.5 point of price and mix is worth about DKK 475m of revenue and about DKK 300-450m of EBIT (2-3%).

What the recent data say about pure price:

  • Price and mix. +0.8% in Q1 2026, +1.0% in H1 2026 (against 1.4% expected), then revenue per hectolitre +3% in Q3, positive in every region: Western Europe +2%, Asia +2%, CEEI +4%.
  • The Q3 jump is partly geographic mix. Chinese revenue per hectolitre is about 30% below the group average (our arithmetic), so a 10% fall in Chinese volume lifts group revenue per hectolitre by roughly 0.5-1 point. Soft-drink growth pulls the other way (Morgan Stanley: soft drinks "partially offset price increases").
  • Our decomposition of Q3: pure price about 2-2.5%, geographic mix about +0.7, category mix about -0.3. In H1, pure price was about 1.3-1.5% after the same adjustments.
  • Full year 2026: price and mix of about 1.8-2.0%.

What the 2027 policy requires. The 2027 cost headwind is about 160bp of gross margin (Industry digest), on input inflation of about 5.4% (Morgan Stanley).

  • Sensitivities: a 10% aluminium rise needs about 1.3% of price (Bernstein); a 10% energy rise needs about 1% (Jefferies).
  • Holding gross profit in money needs about 1.5-1.7% of price on cost alone.
  • Holding gross margin percentage needs about 2.9%.

Management's policy (price with local CPI of about 2-3% in the main markets, more in Laos, Kazakhstan, Ukraine and India; cover more than 80% of input inflation) is consistent with holding gross profit in money and letting the percentage slip. We therefore model 2027 pure price at about 2.5-2.8%, revenue per hectolitre at about 2.0-2.3% after mix (soft drinks about -0.3; China's recovery diluting about -0.2 to -0.3), and gross margin flat to -30bp.

Market by market, where price works and where it does not:

  • Works (strongholds and emerging markets with dominant share):
  • the Nordics and Switzerland (Feldschlösschen);
  • Laos (about 90% share; high-single-digit price rises against inflation);
  • India (state reforms such as Karnataka's alcohol-content tax and price deregulation);
  • Kazakhstan and Ukraine;
  • excise pass-through, normally within months.
  • Contested:
  • UK mainstream lager: Heineken and Molson Coors cut alcohol strength and spent the duty saving on price; Carlsberg chose margin over volume.
  • Polish mainstream: Asahi discounting through the deposit-scheme disruption; Carlsberg's volume down by low double digits in H1 2026.
  • Chinese super-premium: 1664 Blanc -5% in Q3; Chongqing price and mix -60bp in Q3.
  • Concentrated European grocery: the Swiss delisting in Q4 2025; buying alliances.
  • Excise markets:
  • Vietnam's special consumption tax rises about 5 points a year from 2027 (65% to 90% by 2031). Redburn estimates about 3% a year of gross price is needed, with market growth slowing to 2-3%.
  • Excise pass-through raises gross price but adds little to net revenue or margin. It is a volume risk, not a margin source. Malaysia 2005-07 saw volume -5% a year for three years after excise steps.
  • Soft drinks:
  • PepsiCo compounds concentrate price increases of about 4-5% a year (expert call), which caps the bottler's margin unless retail price follows.
  • UK soft-drink price elasticity is low (about -0.5). Sugar-tax pass-through precedents are strong: CCEP's GB price and mix rose about 12.5% in 2018 after the levy.
  • The UK threshold falls to 4.5g per 100ml on 1 January 2028. It is manageable through Pepsi Max, but adds about 8% of the market to the levy's scope.

Price's contribution, line by line:

FY2026E FY2027E FY2028E FY2029E FY2030E
Pure price about 1.9% about 2.6% about 2.5% about 2.4% about 2.4%
Category and geographic mix about +0.1% about -0.5% about -0.2% about -0.2% about -0.2%
Revenue per hectolitre about 2.0% about 2.1% about 2.3% about 2.2% about 2.2%
Organic volume (incl. licence takeovers) about +0.8% about +0.5% about +0.6% about +0.3% (Nordic swap) about +0.6%
Organic revenue about 3.0% about 2.9% about 3.2% about 2.8% (plus about 0.3 from the swap) about 3.2%
Gross margin change (company basis) +0 to +30bp 0 to -30bp +10 to +30bp -10 to 0bp +10 to +30bp

Peers, and why Carlsberg is in the middle.

  • AB InBev earns real pricing (price and mix above inflation) on leadership in 75% of its volume.
  • Heineken deliberately prices below inflation in Europe (Q3 2026 constant-geography European price and mix +0.1%) to win volume back. Its Q3 price and mix slowed to 0.7-1.8% while volume rose 3.9%.
  • Carlsberg leads in only 24% of its volume by weight and is a challenger in 66%. That is why its pricing is nominal: strong where it leads (Nordics, Switzerland, Laos, Western China), weak where it challenges (UK, Poland, Chinese premium).

Heineken's below-CPI stance in Europe is a ceiling on Carlsberg's mainstream pricing in shared markets. Its new chief executive's cost-and-accountability mandate makes a price war less likely than continued price moderation.

Width and skew.

  • 2027 price and mix plausibly lands between 1.5% and 3.0%.
  • Downside (about 25%): price and mix below 2% while input inflation exceeds 5%. EBIT growth falls to about 2.5-3%.
  • Upside (about 20%): price holds at about 3% as competitors also price for costs (the industry generally passes 2027 cost through), giving EBIT growth of about 6.5-7%.

The precedent that warns against assuming the upside is 2011: price taken to cover input costs cost Carlsberg 250bp of volume share in a quarter.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7545P-0145
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

D4523rd of 35

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement. Material and two-edged.

Sensitivity Effect Source
1% change in revenue About 1.5% change in EBIT, with operating costs flat Bernstein
0.5 point change in operating margin About DKK 460m of EBIT, about 3%
2% change in volume About DKK 1.8bn of revenue
1% volume shortfall in Western Europe About DKK 220m of EBIT, about 1.6% of group Bernstein
Chinese volume falls Amplified by under-used breweries (Q2 and Q3 earn about 66% of China's profit) Morgan Stanley

Range. For FY2027, organic revenue of +2% to +5% gives EBIT growth of roughly +3% to +8% before the 2027 cost headwind. That is a cone about 5 points wide around consensus EBIT growth of 5.9%.

Why:

  • Structure. Variable costs (packaging and raw materials, about 55-60% of cost of goods sold) move with volume. Brewery, logistics and overhead costs are largely fixed in the short run.
  • Peer reference. For Heineken, a 2% fall in global beer volume cuts EPS by about 5%.
  • Implication. China is the main source of negative leverage: a 10% Chinese volume fall in the peak quarters costs roughly 1-1.5% of group EBIT (our inference). Multi-beverage density is a slow source of positive leverage.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

D4525th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Operating leverage will shape the line items much less than its textbook size suggests, for two reasons. First, volume, the input that operating leverage multiplies, is close to flat: group organic volume of about 0 to +0.5% a year in our base. Second, Carlsberg manages its operating leverage: it flexes marketing and overheads to land profit guidance, which dampens both the upside and the downside. The leverage that matters for the forecasts is therefore not volume against fixed costs. It is price against cost. A 1% price move falls almost entirely to profit, worth about 6% of EBIT, while a 1% volume move is worth about 1.5-1.8% of EBIT. Three pockets of genuine fixed-cost leverage will move the line items:

  • Chinese breweries. Negative in 2026; positive on easy comparatives from Q2 2027.
  • The new Kazakhstan plant. Positive as utilisation builds in 2027-28.
  • Danish and Finnish lines. Negative around the Coca-Cola to PepsiCo switch in 2029.

Against the last six years, the forecasts change the trend. Over FY2019-25 the EBIT margin on the company's basis went nowhere (about 15.3% in 2019, about 15.4% in 2025 on CPM). We forecast +140bp to 16.8% by 2030. The driver of that change is named cost and perimeter actions, not operating leverage.

The cost structure, and what it implies:

  • Cost of goods sold is about 54-55% of revenue on the company's basis. Of that, materials and packaging are about 57%, which is variable. Indirect production overheads (13%) and depreciation (6%) are fixed in the short run. Direct labour is only 4% (Jefferies, 4 March 2026).
  • Operating expenses are about 28-30% of revenue: distribution 30%, marketing 28%, sales 24%, administration 19%. About half is fixed in the short run.
  • Overall, roughly 35-40% of the cost base is fixed within a year. That gives the sensitivities the sell-side quotes:
  • 1% of revenue moves EBIT about 1.5% with costs flat (Bernstein);
  • operational gearing after mitigation is 1.8x (Jefferies, 2020);
  • 1% of Western European volume is worth about DKK 220m of EBIT.

The record says the leverage is managed, not suffered:

  • 2020 (COVID): organic volume -3.8% and revenue -8.4%, but organic EBIT fell only 3.1%, because marketing, travel and administration were cut 14% organically.
  • 2022-23 (cost spike): cost of sales per hectolitre rose 13% in 2022 and gross margin fell 190bp, then a further 100bp in 2023 to 44.6%, yet organic EBIT grew about 5% in 2023.
  • 2025: volume -2.0%, organic EBIT +5.0%.

So in bad years the company protects profit with opex and accepts a lower gross margin. The leverage shows up in the gross margin line and in brand investment, not in EBIT.

How it shapes each line item:

  • Revenue. No volume-driven acceleration. Reported growth is about 3.1-3.3% a year.
  • Gross margin (company basis 45.2% in 2025, 45.7% in H1 2026). Flat to -30bp in 2027, as cost inflation outruns price. The 47-49% target is not reached by 2028. Soft-drink mix takes about 30bp a year off gross margin percentage, while lowering the cost of serving each outlet.
  • EBIT margin. +45bp in 2026 (synergies), +30bp in 2027, +25bp in 2028, +15bp in 2029 (licence swap), +30bp in 2030.
  • EBITDA margin. It rises faster than the EBIT margin, from 21.0% to 22.4%, because depreciation climbs from about DKK 5.0bn to 5.85bn. Capital expenditure of DKK 6.3-6.5bn a year runs above depreciation (new plants in Kazakhstan, Azerbaijan and India, and Nordic PepsiCo capacity). The forecast shows a capital-intensity effect here: about 15% of EBITDA growth is absorbed by depreciation before it reaches EBIT.

The three fixed-cost pockets, sized:

  1. China. - China is about 14% of revenue and roughly one-fifth of group EBIT (our inference); Q2 and Q3 carry about two-thirds of China's profit. - Chongqing Brewery's Q3 2026: volume -9.6%, gross margin -2.3 points to 48.6%, operating margin -2.5 points to 21.1%, recurring net profit -13.2%. That is roughly 1.4x operating leverage on volume at the gross-profit level, and more at net profit. - For the group, a 10% fall in Chinese peak-season volume costs about 1-1.5% of EBIT. We model China EBIT down about 8-10% in 2026 and up about 3-5% in 2027 (easy comparatives from Q2 2027). - Minority holders own a large part of the Chinese business, so roughly half of any Chinese swing reaches EPS. A DKK 250m swing in Chinese EBIT moves group EPS by about 0.7 DKK (1%), not 1.5 DKK.
  2. Kazakhstan. - The USD 344m plant (1bn litres capacity) opened on 1 October 2026. It replaces toll-filling that made the PepsiCo business loss-making in H1 2026 and took 40bp off the CEEI margin. - We add about DKK 200m of EBIT in 2027 and a further DKK 100-150m in 2028 as utilisation rises (Bernstein: "at least 1 point" of group profit growth in 2027). - Azerbaijan (from 1 January 2027) is about breakeven in its first year (Jefferies) and profitable from 2028.
  3. The Nordic swap. From 1 January 2029, Carlsberg loses its Coca-Cola contracts in Denmark and Finland (about 1% of revenue) and gains PepsiCo there and in the Baltics. Redburn calculates a net loss of about 1.5m hectolitres of Nordic volume. Lines built for Coca-Cola volume will be under-absorbed until PepsiCo volume is built. The precedent: when Coca-Cola stopped Danish-German border trade in 2019, volume on Danish canning lines fell 30%. We take about DKK 150m off 2029 EBIT for the transition.

The cone (FY2027). Around our EBIT of DKK 15.3bn:

  • ±1 point of volume moves EBIT about ±1.5-1.8%;
  • ±0.5 point of price and mix moves EBIT about ±2-3%;
  • ±5 points of Chinese volume moves EBIT about ±0.7%.

The realistic range is DKK 14.9-15.7bn (+2.5% to +7.5% growth). Price against cost is the widest single source of variance, not volume.

Peers.

  • AB InBev runs an EBITDA margin of about 36% and recovered from 33.6% (2023) to 35.8% (2025) through scale and real pricing.
  • Heineken's EverGreen gross savings dropped through at only about 25%.
  • Molson Coors protects margin by cutting marketing.

Carlsberg's pattern sits between these. It protects EBIT growth at about 5% through cost control and accepts a weaker gross margin. That makes EBIT more predictable than the gross margin, and EBIT more predictable than revenue.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7640P-0250
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?F4027th of 35full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

D5020th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Financial leverage helps EPS in FY2026 and FY2027 through falling finance costs, but less than the headline finance-cost guidance suggests. The May 2026 hybrid replaced senior debt with an instrument whose coupon sits below net income, so the combined cost of debt plus hybrid falls only modestly after 2027. From FY2028 the balance sheet stops adding to EPS through interest and starts adding through the share count.

We expect leverage to reach about 2.5x at end-2026 (if Sapporo closes on time) and about 2.4x at end-2027 with a DKK 2bn buyback in H2 2027. Management then holds leverage around 2.2-2.4x and returns DKK 4-5bn a year. Rather than delever towards the pre-Britvic 1.5x, the balance sheet is used to buy back about 2.5-3.5% of the shares a year. That adds about 2.5-3 points to EPS growth in FY2028-30. Financial gearing amplifies EBIT changes by about 1.2x at the pre-tax line (EBIT +5% gives pre-tax profit about +6%), and does the same on the downside.

The balance sheet path (company definition: NIBD including leases and, on our reading, 50% of the hybrid):

End-2025 End-2026E End-2027E End-2028E End-2030E
NIBD (DKK bn) 61.6 about 49 about 49 about 50 about 52
EBITDA (DKK bn) 18.7-18.8 19.6 20.5 21.5 23.4
Leverage 3.28x about 2.5x about 2.4x about 2.3x about 2.2x
Buyback in year (DKK bn) 0 0 2.0 4.0 5.0

How end-2026 gets to 2.5x:

  • the hybrid swap removes about DKK 6.7bn from company-basis NIBD (half of DKK 13.4bn);
  • free cash flow after leases of about DKK 7.2bn, less dividends (DKK 3.8bn), minority dividends (about DKK 0.85bn) and bolt-ons (about DKK 0.6bn), removes about DKK 2bn;
  • Sapporo's USD 643m (about DKK 4.2bn) at closing removes about 0.2x.

Jefferies has 2.46x. If Sapporo slips into 2027, end-2026 is about 2.7x. That is the main reason we date the buyback announcement to the H1 2027 results (August 2027) rather than to February 2027. The India listing is not in the base. Its proceeds (USD 700m maximum secondary sale; about DKK 4-5bn for a 15-25% float) would take about 0.2-0.25x off leverage and most likely fund a larger, earlier buyback.

How it shapes EPS, line by line:

  • Net finance costs.
  • Guided at DKK 1.8bn for 2026 excluding currency; we use about DKK 1.95bn all-in, including currency and other financial items.
  • The cut from DKK 2.2bn came mainly from redeeming bonds with the hybrid proceeds, with only part of a year's effect in 2026, and from moving bank fees above EBIT, which is neutral to earnings.
  • In 2027 three things add about DKK 300m of saving: the full-year effect of the hybrid swap (about DKK 200-250m more), interest saved on the Sapporo cash (about DKK 150m), and organic deleveraging. Refinancing at higher coupons partly offsets them: 18% of gross debt matures by 2028; +100bp on a year of maturities costs 0.8% of pre-tax profit (Morgan Stanley); Deutsche Bank's 6.5% stress costs 1.1% of EPS.
  • After 2027, net debt is held roughly flat by buybacks, so finance costs flatten at about DKK 1.6bn.
  • The hybrid coupon.
  • EUR 1.8bn at 4.37% and 4.875% is about EUR 83m, or DKK 620m gross and DKK 477m after tax.
  • It is cumulative, so it is deducted for EPS as it accrues (about DKK 390m gross for the part of 2026 after issue in May), and in full from 2027.
  • Non-call periods are 5.25 and 8 years, so it is fixed throughout the forecast horizon. Step-up terms after the call dates are undisclosed.
  • Net effect on EPS of the hybrid swap alone. Coupon DKK 620m gross against interest saved on DKK 13.4bn of senior debt at about 3.5% (about DKK 470m) gives net dilution of about DKK 150m pre-tax, about 1.3% of EPS. Bernstein's "about 1%" is right on this net basis. Morgan Stanley's "about 2%" counts the coupon with only part of the interest saving.
  • A modelling trap for the Consumer. A broker who takes the lower finance-cost guidance and does not deduct the coupon overstates FY2027 EPS by about DKK 3.6 (5%). That trap fits the unusual spread of FY2027 broker EPS (DKK 65.57 to 75.00) on revenue estimates within 4% of each other.

The combined financing cost (finance costs plus hybrid coupon, pre-tax): about DKK 2.34bn in 2026, about DKK 2.27bn in 2027, about DKK 2.22bn in 2028, then flat. FY2025 finance costs were DKK 2.64bn on FactSet's net interest line, and about DKK 2.4bn on the adjusted basis our reconstruction of the DKK 60.81 EPS implies. Against that, the whole deleveraging benefit from the Britvic peak is only about DKK 0.1-0.4bn pre-tax, or 1-3% of EPS. The hybrid swap has absorbed most of what deleveraging would otherwise have added to EPS.

EPS contribution of financial leverage by year:

  • FY2026: about +0.6 points net (finance saving about +4.3 points, partial coupon -3.7 points).
  • FY2027: about +0.5 points net (finance saving +2.5 points, full-year coupon -2.0 points).
  • FY2028-30: about +0.3 points a year from finance, plus +2.5 to +3.5 points from buybacks.

Comfort and risk:

  • No covenants bite.
  • The rating is protected by the hybrid's 50% equity credit (Moody's, Fitch).
  • Leverage excluding China was 4.3x in a January 2025 stress (Morgan Stanley), a reminder that China's cash flows support the ratio.

The risk is not solvency. It is that an EBIT stall delays the buyback: Morgan Stanley's bear case of 2% organic EBIT growth stalls deleveraging and the buyback, and de-rates the shares to DKK 780. A one-year delay in the buyback costs about 2.5% of FY2028 EPS.

Balance sheet quality.

  • Book equity rises with the hybrid (DKK 13.4bn) and retained profit. Consensus shows equity falling in FY2027 while debt rises to DKK 63bn, which is internally inconsistent; the Consumer should not use the brokers' balance sheets.
  • Trade working capital is negative (FactSet net working capital -13.5% of revenue at end-2025, -11.7% at the seasonal H1 2026), partly funded by DKK 3.2bn of supplier finance. That is a quiet form of financial leverage that could reverse.

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

A621st of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. About DKK 1.2-1.4bn of identifiable cost and perimeter benefits are available over FY2026-28. About two-thirds of it reaches EBIT, roughly DKK 0.8-0.9bn, equal to about 6% of 2025 EBIT and about 90bp of margin. The rest is reinvested in marketing (8.3% of revenue moving towards 8.5-9%) and in price. The timing is front-loaded:

  • FY2026: about DKK 550-650m (mainly Britvic synergies).
  • FY2027: about DKK 450-550m (last synergies, Kazakhstan in-housing, restructuring paybacks).
  • FY2028: about DKK 200-300m (Kazakhstan and Azerbaijan utilisation, restructuring tails).
  • FY2029: turns negative because of the Nordic swap's transition costs.

Identifiable self-help therefore supplies more than half of EBIT growth in FY2026-27 and about one-sixth in FY2028. That is the central timing insight for the third set: the part of the forecast management most directly controls is largely delivered by end-2027. FY2028-30 rests on price and mix running ahead of costs, plus new efficiency programmes not yet announced. No large cost programme on the scale of Funding the Journey is identifiable. The cost base can be cut further, but most remaining savings are earmarked for reinvestment.

The named levers, sized and dated:

Lever Gross size Timing Reaches EBIT Evidence
Britvic synergies, GBP 110m net of commercial reinvestment (about DKK 950m) About 30% in 2025, about 50% in 2026, about 20% in 2027 DKK 475m in 2026; DKK 190m in 2027; none after Fully, being stated net Integration cost GBP 83m, more than half in year one; ahead of the five-year plan
Kazakhstan: end of toll-filling 40bp CEEI margin drag in H1 2026; PepsiCo business loss-making in H1 About DKK 200m in 2027, DKK 100-150m more in 2028 Largely; some start-up cost in H1 2027 Plant opened 1-Oct-26 on schedule; Redburn gross margin about 35% at scale
Restructurings booked in 2025 (Teisseire DKK 340m, UK DKK 71m plus DKK 199m impairment, Cambodia DKK 84m, Kronenbourg DKK 53m, China DKK 16m) Savings not disclosed; typical payback 2-3 years suggests about DKK 200-300m a year at run-rate 2026-28 About half, the rest offsetting wage inflation Teisseire from a -2% margin; outsourcing of French field sales
Ongoing efficiency ("Funding our Journey" as a standing culture; procurement; digital tools; IT centre in India; G&A flat as a share of revenue) About 1% of the cost base a year Every year Mostly absorbed by wage inflation and marketing step-up 2026 target of flattish cost of sales per hectolitre
Azerbaijan PepsiCo licence Breakeven in 2027 Profit from 2028 About DKK 50-80m in 2028 Jefferies
Nordic Coca-Cola to PepsiCo swap Transition costs (Royal Unibrew's mirror image is DKK 300m) 2028-29 Negative: about -DKK 100m in 2028, -150m in 2029 Redburn: net loss of 1.5m hectolitres

What reaches each line item:

  • Gross margin. Synergies (procurement, logistics) and Kazakhstan sit mainly in cost of sales. They roughly offset the 2027 cost headwind of about 160bp, so gross margin is flat to -30bp in 2027 instead of -100bp or worse.
  • Operating expenses. G&A flat as a share of revenue; marketing up about 10bp a year. Net, opex as a share of revenue falls about 10-20bp a year in 2026-27 and is flat thereafter.
  • EBIT. Self-help supplies about DKK 475m of the DKK 884m increase in FY2026 (54%), about DKK 390-400m of the DKK 728m increase in FY2027 (55%), and about DKK 150m of the DKK 765m increase in FY2028 (20%).
  • Cash. Restructuring cash runs at about DKK 800m in 2026 and about DKK 500m in 2027. CPM excludes it from EBIT, so it shows only in free cash flow. That is about DKK 0.3bn of extra cash a year by 2027-28 as it fades.

Precedents and how much drops through:

  • Funding the Journey (2015-19). About DKK 3bn of gross savings against a DKK 1.5-2.0bn target; about 40% reinvested (DKK 940m of DKK 2.3bn by early 2018); SG&A fell 260bp of revenue; EBIT margin went from 12.9% to 15.9% over four years. That was a much larger programme, run from a lower margin, when leadership was cost-first. The current culture is moving to revenue-weighted incentives.
  • Heineken. Over EUR 3bn of gross savings since 2019, with only about 25% dropping through to operating profit because of inflation and reinvestment.
  • AB InBev. Cut sales and marketing by 530bp of revenue after SABMiller, but recovered EBITDA margin only slowly (43.1% in 2016, 33.6% in 2023, 35.8% in 2025).
  • Molson Coors. Delivered USD 150m six months early (2012) and now targets USD 450m over 2026-28, partly by cutting marketing.

The pattern says a programme's drop-through falls when the company is also trying to grow revenue. Carlsberg is now in that position, so we assume about 65% drop-through, not Funding the Journey's 60% plus a margin-first culture.

What would change the timing:

  • Upside: a new efficiency programme announced at a capital markets day (we think one is likely by 2028, with a new strategy cycle); Kazakhstan reaching group-average margins in 2027 rather than 2028.
  • Downside: marketing stepping up faster towards 9% under the growth culture; special items not narrowing; 2027 cost inflation forcing the savings to be spent on price rather than kept.
5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4752P-0556
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?D5219th of 35full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

C+5611th of 35

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Two reinforcing loops matter for the forecasts:

  • Deleveraging to buybacks: positive, but rate-limited.
  • PepsiCo route density: positive over three to five years, but with balancing dips at each licence transition.

One negative loop is running now: Chinese volume, then destocking, then brewery under-absorption, then defensive marketing. It is likely to turn from reinforcing to balancing in 2027 as comparatives ease, because it has a natural brake.

Two balancing loops cap the upside:

  • Soft-drink mix. Each step of soft-drink growth lowers revenue per hectolitre and gross margin percentage even as it lowers the cost of serving each outlet.
  • Capital recycling through minority sales (Sapporo, India). Cash now, leakage later.

The loop most likely to surprise in either direction is the culture shift from cost to revenue incentives. Evidence of it is thin either way.

Net effect on the line items: the loops support EBIT growth of about 5% and EPS growth of about 8% a year. They do not create a self-accelerating case. The only loop that compounds EPS mechanically is the buyback, and its scale is set by free cash flow of about DKK 2.5-4.5bn a year after dividends, not by the share price.

Loop 1: deleveraging, then lower interest, then buyback, then EPS accretion, then re-rating.

  • How it works now. The first leg, deleveraging lowering interest, has been weakened by the hybrid: interest saved is largely paid out as coupon. So the loop now runs through the buyback leg alone. Leverage below 2.5x (about end-2026 if Sapporo closes; end-2027 at the latest) opens DKK 2-5bn a year of buybacks, 1.5-3.5% of shares at about DKK 1,000.
  • The re-rating leg is unreliable. Brewers rarely re-rate on cost alone while volume falls, and October's re-rating came from a sector rotation, not from estimates.
  • Precedents:
  • AB InBev: leverage from 5.5x to 2.87x; a USD 6bn buyback; consensus met or beaten in 15 of 17 quarters; shares +27-35% in twelve months.
  • Carlsberg 2015-19: cost reset, leverage down, cash returns doubled, multiple to the high teens.
  • What stops it: EBIT growth below about 3% (Morgan Stanley's 2% bear case); Sapporo slipping; supplier-finance reversal; large M&A (management keeps flexibility, and reaching 2.5x "will not automatically trigger" buybacks, per Jefferies); a sharp rise in refinancing rates.
  • In the forecasts: buybacks of DKK 2bn in H2 2027, DKK 4bn in 2028, DKK 4.5bn in 2029 and DKK 5bn in 2030. The share count falls from 132.4m to about 118.5m by end-2030. That is about 2.5 points of EPS growth a year from FY2028.

Loop 2: multi-beverage density, in which each PepsiCo licence lowers the cost of serving the next and makes PepsiCo award more.

  • Evidence it runs:
  • the footprint grows from 5 PepsiCo markets in 2024 to 17 by January 2029;
  • the Nordic model has run above group margins for about 25 years;
  • integrated capex is about half that of a standalone bottler (Jefferies).
  • The balancing dips:
  • Kazakhstan was loss-making on toll-filling in H1 2026;
  • Azerbaijan is breakeven in 2027;
  • the Nordic swap brings a net 1.5m hectolitre loss and transition costs in 2028-29.
  • The reverse loop is real. Losing San Miguel in the UK cost 2-3% of EBIT. Royal Unibrew's loss of PepsiCo costs it about 13% of sales and up to 23% of EBIT before mitigation.
  • In the forecasts: reported revenue gets about +0.3 points a year from licence takeovers, counted as organic. EBIT gets +DKK 200m in 2027, +DKK 150m in 2028 and -DKK 150m in 2029. Capex stays at DKK 6.3-6.5bn, so depreciation rises.
  • What would strengthen it: a new territory such as Poland (Jefferies: "not guaranteed"). DNB Carnegie puts each mid-sized territory at +4-8% to EPS over time, which we treat as aggressive.
  • What would weaken it: Royal Unibrew taking the Danish and Finnish Coca-Cola licences, which would strengthen a rival's route density in Carlsberg's home market.

Loop 3: Chinese volume falls, distributors destock, breweries under-absorb, marketing rises, premium share comes under pressure.

  • Evidence it is reinforcing now:
  • China volume -6% in Q2 and about -10% in Q3; Chongqing Brewery's Q3: volume -9.6%, gross margin -2.3 points, recurring net profit -13.2%, with selling expenses kept high to defend premium;
  • 1664 Blanc -5% in Q3;
  • Budweiser APAC: China volume -16.8% and organic EBITDA -30.5% in Q3, the extreme version of the loop.
  • Heineken's licensed volume through China Resources Beer grew in the mid-twenties. That shows part of the loss is share and channel (nightlife, on-trade), not only weather.
  • The brakes:
  • comparatives ease from Q2 2027;
  • Jefferies reports inventories back to normal at end-Q3;
  • the Carlsberg brand grew double digits in China in Q3;
  • Tsingtao's mid-to-high-end volume grew 2% in the first nine months;
  • stimulus could restore on-trade traffic.
  • In the forecasts: China EBIT -8 to -10% in 2026, +3-5% in 2027, +2-3% a year thereafter. Because minority holders take roughly half of Chinese profit, the loop's effect on EPS is about half its effect on EBIT.
  • What would make it structural: Chongqing's mainstream brands (Chongqing, Wusu) losing share in Western China to China Resources Beer pricing. That is the low-probability, high-impact case (about -4% of group EBIT for a 20% fall in China profit).

Loop 4: soft-drink mix growth dilutes revenue per hectolitre and gross margin while improving route density.

  • Soft drinks are about 33% of volume (16% before Britvic), sell at about a 20% discount to the group average, and take about 30bp a year off price and mix (Redburn).
  • They also lower the cost of serving each outlet and the capital intensity of distribution. The Nordic integrated operations run above group margins.
  • Net: a balancing loop on gross margin percentage and revenue per hectolitre, and a mildly reinforcing loop on absolute EBIT and EBIT margin. In the forecasts, gross margin is held flat to slightly down while EBIT margin rises about 30bp a year. Redburn sees the UK margin rising from 12% (2025) to 15% (2029).

Loop 5: capital recycling through minority sales.

  • Sapporo pays 21.3x EBIT for 25% of Southeast Asia and Hong Kong, while Carlsberg trades at about 12.5x earnings. The proceeds (about DKK 4.2bn) cut leverage by about 0.2x.
  • But the minority takes about DKK 150m of net income a year, against interest saved of about DKK 113m after tax: about -0.4% to EPS until the cash is bought back.
  • An India float (25-40%) would repeat the trade: proceeds of DKK 4-5bn, a new minority charge of about DKK 80-150m (Bernstein: DKK 80m for 40%), and about 2% EPS accretion only if the proceeds fund buybacks (Redburn).
  • This loop converts growth assets into buyback capacity. It is EPS-neutral to mildly positive, but it lowers the share of group profit that belongs to Carlsberg's shareholders from the fastest-growing regions. In the forecasts, minorities rise from about DKK 1.18bn (2026) to about DKK 1.34bn (2027) and DKK 1.48bn (2030), growing faster than EBIT.

Loop 6: culture shift from cost discipline to revenue-weighted incentives.

  • Pay was rewired towards revenue and the short-term bonus cap was raised to 150% of salary. Diageo's and Heineken's experiences show such shifts can erode cost discipline: SG&A creep, marketing increases that do not lift volume.
  • Evidence of erosion so far is thin. Opex fell about 1% organically in H1 2026; marketing is 8.3% against a 9% framework; whistleblower reports rose 46%.
  • In the forecasts: marketing rises about 10bp a year. A faster step to 9% would cost about DKK 650m, or 4% of EBIT.

Loop 7: the guidance culture. Conservative initial ranges, then beats, then credibility. It now works as a balancing loop on estimates: consensus sits at the guidance midpoint and the sell-side expects the habitual beat, so beats no longer create upgrades. For the forecasts this means delivery risk below our EBIT numbers is lower than the cost and China arithmetic alone implies, because management manages to the number.

Guidance is specific, conservative by habit and reliable on profit:

  • organic EBIT at or above the top of the range nearly every year;
  • upward narrowing at every half year since 2023.

For FY2026 it is in line with consensus at the midpoint, and the expected beat is about 1%. The beat is shrinking and no longer drives upgrades. Adjusted for the synergy tailwind, FY2026 guidance implies only 1-3% underlying growth, a sign of how much China and Kazakhstan are costing. C-68

To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

C5212th of 35

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement. For FY2026, guidance is in line with consensus, not above it.

  • Operating profit. On the restated CPM base of DKK 13,686m, consensus EBIT of DKK 14,472m equals the guidance midpoint of 5% organic growth plus the guided +DKK 100m of translation. Given the company's record of landing at the top of its range, there is upside of about 1% to FY2026 EBIT, about DKK 130-150m, and about 1% to EPS.
  • Other lines. Net finance costs (DKK 1.8bn), tax (about 23%) and capital expenditure (DKK 6-7bn; consensus DKK 6.5bn) are also in line.

For FY2027 there is no formal guidance until 9 February 2027. The mid-term framework (4-6% organic revenue growth, operating profit growing faster, leverage below 2.5x by end-2027) implies organic EBIT growth of about 5-8%. Consensus FY2027 EBIT growth of +5.9% (EPS +6.5%) sits in the lower half of that implied range.

Why consensus sits there:

  • Brokers haircut the revenue algorithm: consensus revenue growth is +3.8%, against a 4-6% target.
  • They pencil in 2027 input inflation, the full-year hybrid coupon and the Sapporo minority.

The risk on 9 February. If management repeats its usual pattern of a wide initial range (2026 opened at 2-6%), the midpoint could sit below consensus. History says brokers then hold numbers in anticipation of the usual beat, so the risk is to sentiment more than to estimates.

C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

F4526th of 35

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement. FY2026 guidance is similar to recent delivery (6.0% in 2024, 5.0% in 2025), but adjusting for input forces changes the reading.

  • The help. About 3 points of growth comes from Britvic synergies (about 50% of GBP 110m, about DKK 450-475m, in 2026). Cost of goods sold per hectolitre is flattish. Currency has turned positive.
  • The drags. China, Kazakhstan toll-filling, and marketing reinvestment.

So the guidance implies only about 1-3% underlying organic EBIT growth excluding synergies: conservative relative to the help, and realistic given China.

Mid-term framework. It is not conservative against history or against the drags:

  • the ten-year organic revenue growth rate is 3.1%;
  • embedded beer volume growth is 0.3%;
  • Kazakhstan laps in 2027;
  • the synergy tailwind falls to about 1.4 points in 2027 and to zero in 2028.

The revenue ambition is aggressive. The EBIT ambition (faster than revenue) is moderate. The leverage target is conservative: it is likely to be reached by mid-2027, before end-2027.

For the model. FY2027 organic EBIT growth of 5-6% in the central case and 7-8% in the upside (Kazakhstan, China comparatives, pricing). The downside is 3-4% if 2027 costs are not priced.

To what extent is informal/qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

D4020th of 35

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement. Six items:

  1. Early adoption of IFRS 18 with Carlsberg's own performance measures. The 2025 base was restated (DKK 13,686m against 13,996m), special items were removed, bank fees moved above operating profit and associates below it. FactSet's standardised history differs from CPM by about 15%. Comparisons break for a year.
  2. The hybrid's equity treatment. It makes reported leverage (3.0x against FactSet's 3.59x) and finance costs look better, while the coupon still dilutes EPS.
  3. Reporting cadence. Only revenue and volume are given at Q1 and Q3; profit is given half-yearly. Licence takeovers count as organic, which flatters organic volume (Kazakhstan).
  4. Foundation control. No takeover premium. The investor-relations team is stable (Peter Kondrup, Iben Steiness).
  5. Extra disclosure sources: - Chongqing Brewery is a listed subsidiary, giving separate Chinese disclosure. - The Indian prospectus, if made public, would give rich disclosure. - Associates' volumes (Super Bock, Chinese associates) are excluded from group volume.
  6. FactSet's broker table has a known column shift, so rely on its named rows only.
