Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

C5213th of 35

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector have outperformed and underperformed, and does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement. In European brewers, outperformance has come from three patterns, and underperformance from one:

  1. Self-help margin expansion with buybacks at a low multiple. Carlsberg itself in 2015-19: "Funding the Journey" delivered DKK 3bn of savings against a DKK 2bn target, took the EBIT margin from 12.9% to 15.9%, ran annual buybacks, and the multiple rose towards the high teens. The ten-year median is 16.9x.
  2. Deleveraging that unlocks buybacks, with volume stabilising. AB InBev in 2025-26: - leverage from 3.4x to 2.86x; - a USD 6bn buyback; - consensus met or beaten in 15 of 17 quarters; - shares up 27-35% in twelve months.
  3. Cost programmes plus buybacks compounding EPS at about 12% a year from a multiple near a ten-year low, which pays without a re-rating (Sector digest, October 2026).
  4. Underperformance: volume falls and the premium engine stalls, worse with high leverage. - Heineken 2019-25: EPS compounded at only 1.4% a year and the shares de-rated. - Diageo 2023-26: over-premiumisation, a price reset, a fall in cash conversion and a dividend cut. - Royal Unibrew 2026: losing its PepsiCo licence left it at the 1st percentile of its multiple, down 11% in twelve months.

Rules of thumb:

  • Brewers rarely re-rate on cost alone when volume is falling.
  • Restarting buybacks after M&A deleveraging is a reliable catalyst.
  • Losing a licence is a de-rating event.
  • China exposure drives volatility (Budweiser APAC).

Does Carlsberg fit? It fits pattern 2 on the balance sheet but not on volume. Its EPS compounding of about 8-9% falls short of pattern 3's 12%. It carries some of the pattern 4 risk through China and mainstream beer.

For the Consumer. The realistic 18-month route to outperformance is a buyback restart plus 8-9% EPS growth at a below-median multiple. A re-rating towards the ten-year median of 16.9x would need volume proof that is not in sight.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

B587th of 35

Judgement. On unchanged earnings, about 10-20% to a fair trough multiple. In a stress case with an earnings cut, about 25-30%. The calculation uses consensus EPS 24 months forward of about DKK 74.6, which rolls towards FY2028's DKK 76 within months.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

D4620th of 35

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative ahead is a dated, mostly known sequence of company-controlled steps: in-house production, the Sapporo cash, Azerbaijan, leverage below 2.5x and the buyback restart, then the Coke-to-Pepsi swap. The market already discusses all of them, and consensus carries all except the buyback. The step most likely to move the shares is the buyback announcement, in February or more likely August 2027. The novel swing factor in either direction is China's 2027 print against easy comparatives.

The narrative lacks a demand inflection. The October rerating already paid for part of the balance-sheet story, so the staircase now supports holding the multiple more than expanding it.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–72

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The two sets are congruent on the shape of the case and on every operating input, and where they disagreed the second set was right and the new AlphaSense evidence confirms it. Both say the same thing in different words: a self-help and capital-return compounder with no demand growth, whose profit is reliable (guidance beaten in nearly every year since 2016), whose revenue runs below its own ambition (ten-year organic rate 3.1% against a 4-6% target), whose EPS is cleaner in total than in composition, and whose swing factors are 2027 price against cost, China's 2027 print, and the buyback date. The first set's steer (revenue 3.5-4%, EPS 8-9% a year before buybacks, China as a range, buyback from mid-2027) and the second set's build (revenue about 3%, EPS about 6% before buybacks and 7.5-8% with them, China -7% then +2%, DKK 2bn buyback in H2 2027) are the same case with the second set's arithmetic. Congruence is high on direction and medium on the EPS rate, and the four points where the sets jar all resolve the same way: towards the lower, better-evidenced number.

Where the sets jar, and how each resolves:

  1. EPS compounding: 8-9% before buybacks (first set) against about 6% (second set). The gap is below EBIT: the first set put "minorities plus hybrid" at about DKK 0.9bn in FY2026, implying minorities of about DKK 0.6bn; the second set reconstructed minorities at DKK 1.15-1.18bn. The own-1 report settles it: reported minorities were DKK 1,023m in FY2025 and DKK 658m in H1 2026, and DNB Carnegie models DKK 1,073m, 1,293m and 1,367m for FY2026-28. The first set's figure was about half the true size. The second set's number was slightly high (we lower it to DKK 1,100m for FY2026 in the read-first section), but its conclusion stands: EPS compounds at about 6% a year before buybacks, about 8% with them. The memo's modal path of 9.2% a year (FY2027 DKK 70.7, FY2029 DKK 84.4) is about 1-1.5 points a year too high and should be retired.

  2. Leverage and the hybrid: "the company's 3.0x is flattered because it excludes the hybrid" (first set) against "the company's NIBD already carries 50% of the hybrid" (second set). The own-2 report confirms the second set: the H1 2026 interim report adds DKK 6.72bn (50% of the EUR 1.8bn) to NIBD to match Moody's and Fitch, and NIBD includes IFRS 16 lease liabilities (DKK 2.91bn at end-2025). The company's 3.0x is a conservative, rating-agency-consistent figure. This matters for the fourth set: the path to about 2.5x at end-2026 is real, not cosmetic, and the FactSet 3.59x (100% of the hybrid on a lower EBITDA) should not be used.

  3. The hybrid's EPS cost: "about 2 points off FY2027 EPS growth" (first set, from Morgan Stanley) against "net dilution of about 1.3%" (second set, nearer Bernstein). Both are right on their own definitions. The gross coupon (DKK 620m, DKK 477m after tax) is about 5% of net income, which is Morgan Stanley's 2% growth drag when only part of the interest saving is netted. The interest saved on the DKK 12.2bn of senior bonds redeemed in H1 2026 (about DKK 430m a year at the 3.5% average cost of debt) nets the dilution to about 1-1.3%. The own-2 report adds a detail the sets lacked: the legacy bonds stayed outstanding for only about six weeks after the hybrid was issued, so most of the redemption saving is already in 2026's DKK 1.8bn guidance, and the incremental 2027 saving from the swap alone is only about DKK 50-150m. The second set's 2027 all-in finance cost of DKK 1,650m therefore depends more on the Sapporo cash (about DKK 150m of interest saved) and organic deleveraging than on the hybrid swap. It is achievable, and Morgan Stanley's FY2027 net financials of DKK 1,510m supports it, but it sits DKK 150-300m below DNB Carnegie (1,944), Rothschild & Co Redburn (1,850), Deutsche Bank (1,821) and Jefferies (1,800 excluding currency). This is the one place where our build is more optimistic than the sell-side, and it offsets our higher minorities almost exactly at the EPS line.

  4. The Sapporo minority: DKK 150m of net income a year (both sets) against DKK 40m (Bernstein). The sale price implies the joint venture's 2025 EBIT at about USD 121m (USD 643m for 25% at 21.3x), so 25% of EBIT is about USD 30m, about DKK 200m before tax and DKK 150m after. Bernstein's DKK 40m rests on "contributed countries" net profit of DKK 120m, which implies a perimeter that excludes, or already nets out, the part-owned Laotian and Malaysian subsidiaries. The two figures cannot both be right about the same perimeter, and the perimeter is not disclosed. We take DKK 100m. The difference between the extremes is DKK 0.8 a share (about 1%): not a thesis-level item, but one the memo should carry as an open question (research request OWN-1).

Other tensions the memo Analyst should know about, which are not contradictions between the sets:

  • The first set's two buyback dates. C-19 said "roughly even odds in February 2027, likely by August 2027"; C-22 and the second set said August. Management has now settled it: on the Q3 call (29 October 2026) it said reaching 2.5x "will not automatically trigger" a buyback and that an announcement at the full-year results is unlikely; Bernstein and Jefferies date the earliest start to mid-2027. The only accelerant is an Indian listing (SEBI approved the confidential pre-filing on 1 October 2026; an updated draft prospectus was filed on 27 October; market chatter points to a December launch). The sets converge on August 2027, with India as the upside.
  • The guideline fair multiple (15.27x in the model) against the first set's bound of "low-to-mid teens, five-year median 13.8x". The first set argued that the ten-year median of 16.9x belonged to a company with 1.7x leverage, annual buybacks and a Chinese growth story, and that a re-rating needs volume proof. The model's 15.27x sits between the five-year median (13.8x) and the five-year 75th percentile (15.76x). This is a judgement for topic #11; D-07 below bears on it, because the multiple is mostly sector-driven.
  • The first set's "no room for upgrades" (C-13) against its "any beat on 2026 guidance is an unpriced positive" (C-21). Both hold: the beat is worth about 1% of FY2026 EBIT against FactSet and about nil against Bloomberg (read-first, point 6).
  • Free cash flow: "consensus is generous" (first set) against "we are at or above consensus on FactSet's definition" (second set). The second set's definitional explanation (FactSet's FCF is before leases, the hybrid coupon and minority dividends, together about DKK 2.5-2.7bn a year) resolves it; the first set's instinct that distributable cash is lower than it looks was right for the wrong reason.

Why the congruence is real rather than cosmetic. The two sets were written by analysts working from the same sources, so agreement could be echo. The test is whether the new, independent evidence in the AlphaSense reports contradicts either set, and on the main points it does not: the profit record (beats of 300-870bp since 2016, T-I1), the revenue shortfall (2.4% in 2024, -0.6% in 2025, 2.7% in H1 2026, 3.0% in Q3, T-D1), the 2027 cost headwind (Morgan Stanley's 160bp, own-4), the Kazakhstan step (toll-filling cost 40bp of CEEI margin in H1 2026, own-6), the buyback timing (own-5), the hybrid's treatment (own-2) and the minorities (own-1) all land where the sets put them. The one body of evidence that reads more bullishly than either set is the sell-side's EPS compounding (Bernstein's 13% a year to 2029; Jefferies' "double-digit TSR"); the own-1 bridge shows those rates need the buyback and India, and we treat them as the upside case.

What this means for predictability. A case whose two independent builds agree on direction and inputs, and whose disagreements have been resolved by evidence rather than by assertion, is more predictable than one still arguing with itself. The residual uncertainty is in the composition of EPS below EBIT (finance costs, minorities, the Sapporo perimeter), which is exactly where the sell-side also disperses (FY2027 EPS DKK 65.57 to 75.79 on revenue within 4%). The sets are congruent; the market is not.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

C4813th of 35

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement. The sell-side narrative knows the shape of the case and most of its parts; it mis-models the below-operating-profit mechanics in the direction that overstates near-term EPS, and it has not articulated the post-2027 fade of self-help or the arithmetic by which the hybrid coupon absorbs the deleveraging benefit. Of the eleven dynamics tested, seven are widely discussed (Britvic synergies and the Kazakhstan step; the buyback's timing; whether China is weather or share; whether 2027 pricing covers costs; the Indian listing; the Nordic switch; the soft-drink mix effect), three are discussed by one to three houses (the hybrid coupon offsetting the fall in finance costs: Jefferies, Morgan Stanley, Bernstein; the size and rise of minorities: DNB Carnegie, Rothschild & Co Redburn, Bernstein; the IFRS 18 basis differences: Bernstein, Morgan Stanley, Deutsche Bank), and one is discussed by one house only (the incentive shift towards revenue and its effect on cost discipline: Jefferies). What no house articulates: that the EPS benefit of deleveraging is largely consumed by the hybrid until buybacks start, that FY2027 is a pinch year in composition, that self-help supplies more than half of FY2026-27 EBIT growth and about a sixth of FY2028's, and that minorities are proportionally the largest among the European brewers. The investor narrative is thinner than the sell-side's and, on the evidence available, is dominated by three ideas: a cheap defensive brewer (the October rotation bought it without estimate support), a multi-beverage pivot now accepted as the right choice (Bernstein: "most investors now accept the acquisition of Britvic"), and a buyback that is coming; with China as the worry and organic volume as "a key debate". The AlphaSense report found no social-media or fund-letter commentary, so the investor side rests on broker descriptions of investor views and on price behaviour. On balance the dynamics that would surprise the market in the next 18 months are better known on the upside (the buyback, the Kazakhstan step) than on the downside (the pinch-year composition), which is the wrong way round for an investor entering now.

Coverage check. FactSet holds nineteen brokers' FY2027 EPS estimates. The AlphaSense report on the narrative cited six houses' own documents (Jefferies, Bernstein, Morgan Stanley, Rothschild & Co Redburn, DNB Carnegie, Deutsche Bank), which are the houses that publish the most on Carlsberg and whose views set the narrative; the report did not name the houses it left out or state a count, so its "eighty percent" coverage claim is unverified. The thirteen other brokers (Barclays, Jyske Bank, Oddo BHF, Nordea, UBS, Goldman Sachs, Sydbank, CIC, Morningstar, Berenberg, ABG Sundal Collier and two unnamed) are represented only by their numbers. The judgements below credit a view to a house only where its own document was cited.

Dynamic by dynamic:

Dynamic Who discusses it, since when, with what view How well known What the narrative misses
(a) Britvic synergies and the Kazakhstan step in 2026-27 profit growth, and what replaces them after 2027 All six: synergies at about 80% by end-2026 (Jefferies 29 October; Bernstein; Deutsche Bank); Kazakhstan as "a significant 2027 profit step-up" (Jefferies 14 September and 29 October; Morgan Stanley: a step-up to 10% organic EBIT growth in 2027; Rothschild & Co Redburn: 35% gross margin on DKK 1,450m). The post-2027 question is raised only obliquely: Jefferies (14 September) says growth must transition to organic volume drivers "after near-term synergies fade" and "the pace of soft drink consolidation will naturally slow"; Bernstein (30 October) says "organic volume growth remains a key debate" Widely known for 2026-27; thinly known for 2028 No house quantifies that self-help supplies more than half of FY2026-27 EBIT growth and about a sixth of FY2028's, or what the FY2028-30 engine is
(b) Whether the hybrid coupon offsets the fall in net finance costs Jefferies (29 June, 28 September 2026): "entirely offset", DKK 475m sits below non-controlling interests; Morgan Stanley (30 June, 26 August): about 2% dilutive from 2027; Bernstein (15 July): about 1% dilutive; the AlphaSense report concludes "most consensus models ignore this reclassification" Known to three houses; mis-modelled by others The combined cost of debt plus hybrid falls only about DKK 70m from 2026 to 2027; the interest leg of the deleveraging loop is largely spent until buybacks start
(c) The size of minorities and how Sapporo and India change them DNB Carnegie (DKK 1,073m, 1,293m, 1,367m); Rothschild & Co Redburn (Sapporo "1% dilutive in the outer years"; India creates a new minority); Bernstein (Sapporo DKK 40m; India "permanently reduces long-term upside"); Jefferies ("modestly positive to neutral") Known as a line; not known as a scale That Carlsberg's minorities are 14.65% of consolidated profit against Heineken's 11.8%; that the Sapporo charge is disputed by a factor of four; that minorities rise faster than EBIT
(d) The timing and size of a buyback restart All six; since July 2026 (Bernstein, 6 July: Sapporo "brings a resumption of buybacks closer"); consensus view now "unlikely at the full-year results, H2 2027" (Jefferies 29 October; Bernstein 30 October); size: Jefferies' illustrative DKK 6-10bn for 2027 (aggressive), others unquantified; consensus share count flat at 132m (DNB Carnegie keeps it flat "until 2027") Widely known in timing; not in the models The date is known; the accretion is not in consensus; the Foundation's pro rata participation and the 2.5x "fit for the business" comment (so leverage will be held, not driven lower) are noted by few
(e) Whether 2027 pricing covers 2027 input costs Bernstein (13 August: aluminium a 27% headwind, about 2% of extra price needed; 30 October: management "confident its pricing toolkits can fully cope"); Jefferies (29 October: a "less helpful cost backdrop", elasticity risk in Western Europe); Morgan Stanley (160bp commodity margin impact); DNB Carnegie (downside if costs cannot be passed on) Widely known as a risk The distinction between covering cost in money (demonstrated) and holding gross margin (not), and that the 47-49% target is incompatible with 2027
(f) Whether China's 2026 fall is weather and destocking or share loss All six, split: management and Rothschild & Co Redburn (weather, destocking, "cleaner inventories" for 2027); Jefferies (no V-shape; on-trade structural); DNB Carnegie (austerity and sentiment); Morgan Stanley (Heineken via China Resources Beer +mid-twenties; Chongqing -9.6%); the own-3 report: expert consensus leans to consumer confidence rather than weather Widely known; unresolved That about half of any Chinese swing never reaches EPS (minorities), so the share-price sensitivity to China exceeds the EPS sensitivity
(g) The Coca-Cola to PepsiCo switch in 2029 Rothschild & Co Redburn (30 September: net -1.5m hectolitres, -DKK 1,416m of sales, -DKK 496m of gross profit, -3.3% of operating profit); Jefferies (DKK 300m of transition costs; "removes a major overhang"); DNB Carnegie (Royal Unibrew's mirror image); Bernstein (Pepsi "a distant number two") Known in detail at one house, in outline elsewhere Outside the 18-month window; the 2028 planning costs and Danish and Finnish line under-utilisation are not in 2028 estimates
(h) The Indian listing's status, size and use of proceeds All six: up to USD 700m, 86.42m shares, an offer for sale, SEBI approval 1 October, about 2% EPS accretion if bought back (Rothschild & Co Redburn, Deutsche Bank); use of proceeds uncommitted Widely known Nothing material; the market treats it as an option, as we do
(i) Soft-drink mix on revenue per hectolitre and gross margin Rothschild & Co Redburn (a 20% discount to beer, -30bp a year of price and mix); Morgan Stanley (soft drinks "partially offset price increases"); Jefferies (Nordic combined operations run above group margins) Known That the mix effect and the 47-49% gross-margin target cannot both be true by 2028
(j) The shift of incentives to revenue growth and its effect on cost discipline Jefferies only (14 September 2026): "investors worry cost discipline will be compromised for volume"; the counter-view that the under-promise culture persists Known to one house The two observable tests (marketing ratio above 8.5%; special items not narrowing) are not tracked
(k) IFRS 18 and CPM restatement, and the basis differences across data providers Bernstein (19 August: "difficult to compare P&L items below operating profit"); Morgan Stanley (CPM base about 2% lower); Deutsche Bank ("temporary modelling friction"); Jefferies (DKK 0.4bn moved to EBIT) Known as friction, not as a cause of the EPS spread That FactSet's FY2026 EPS growth of +44% and its FY2026 EBIT median at the guidance midpoint (against Bloomberg at 5.8%) are basis artefacts that will confuse the February 2027 reaction

The investor narrative, as far as it can be seen. No investor commentary (social media, fund letters, holder statements) was retrieved, so this rests on (1) brokers' descriptions of investor views and (2) price behaviour. Brokers describe investors as having accepted Britvic, as debating organic volume growth, as worried about cost discipline under the new incentives (Jefferies), and as waiting for the buyback. Price behaviour says the marginal buyer in October was a sector-rotation buyer (brewers +9.6-17.4% without estimate support; Carlsberg +14.1% with target prices -0.2%) and the marginal seller on 29 October was reacting to volume and China (-3%). The buy-side narrative is therefore simpler than the sell-side's: "cheap, defensive, multi-beverage, buyback coming, China the risk". None of the below-the-line mechanics appears in it. That absence is the point: an investor who buys on "buyback coming and EPS compounding at double digits" (Bernstein's 13% a year to 2029 is the most-quoted bullish rate) will meet, in February 2027, a P&L in which the coupon is shown, the opening range is below consensus and EPS growth for 2027 is 6% on our arithmetic.

Peers and the sector, for what investors are focused on. Across the same houses, the sector narrative is: valuation support ("plenty of valuation support and solid EPS growth but no re-rating, yet", Bernstein, 12 October); rising bond yields and refinancing risk (Deutsche Bank, 27 October: Carlsberg's third-year EPS sensitivity -1.1% at a 6.5% refinancing rate, among the lowest); weak China (every brewer's Q3); the 2027 cost upswing (Bernstein's August note); and the defensive rotation. Investors in the sector are paying for volume momentum (AB InBev, Heineken's +3.9%) and for buyback execution (AB InBev's USD 6bn, Heineken's EUR 1.5bn), and are not paying for cost-led stories with falling volume (Diageo, Pernod Ricard, Royal Unibrew). Carlsberg sits between: a cost-led story with a buyback coming, which is why its narrative hinges on the August 2027 announcement.

How well known, by horizon. For the next 18 months, the upside dynamics are well known (the buyback's date, the Kazakhstan step, the Chinese comparatives), and the downside composition is not (the hybrid, minorities, the pinch year), so the narrative is more likely to be surprised negatively in the spring than positively; the buyback, when it comes, will confirm rather than surprise, and its EPS accretion (not in consensus) is the one under-known positive. For 18 months to three years, the under-known items are the fade of named self-help after 2027, the 2029 Nordic swap's 2028 planning costs, and the rising minorities; the over-known item is the double-digit EPS compounding, which needs India and a DKK 4-5bn buyback every year.

Our assessment against the narrative, dynamic by dynamic: synergies and Kazakhstan, aligned; the hybrid offset, we are more cautious than the narrative; minorities, more cautious; the buyback's date, aligned, its accretion, we are ahead of consensus; 2027 pricing, aligned on the risk, more cautious on the margin; China, aligned (a range), with a smaller EPS sensitivity than the narrative implies; the Nordic swap, aligned with Rothschild & Co Redburn; India, aligned; soft-drink mix, aligned; incentives, aligned with Jefferies; IFRS 18, we are more worried about the February 2027 reaction than the narrative is.

Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 29 October 2026, 22:51 to 23:11 UK, Opus, richie login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Carlsberg (CARL.B-DK): answers to the first set of questions

Written 29 October 2026 for the Investment Analyst who builds the operating and financial model. Share price DKK 934.2 on 29 October 2026, down 3.0% on the day of the Q3 2026 trading statement (previous close DKK 963.4).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Read first: the facts, bases and reconciliations the model must hold fixed

  1. Which results the sources cover. The "latest" Earnings Review (18 September 2026) covers H1 2026, the period to 30 June 2026, reported on 19 August 2026. That matches the FactSet block, whose last period is H1 2026, so the caution in the instruction is resolved. The Q3 2026 trading statement (29 October 2026) came after the Earnings Review and appears only in the Change Forces report. The latest memo (1 October 2026) was written at DKK 840.6. The share price is now 11% higher.
  2. Q3 2026 in brief. Organic revenue grew 3.0%, against consensus of 2.6%. Total volume fell 0.4%, against consensus growth of 0.6%; organic volume rose 0.3%. Revenue per hectolitre rose 3%, and it rose in every region. Reported revenue was DKK 25,281m. In Central and Eastern Europe and India (CEEI), organic revenue grew 11.7% and volume 7.5%, with soft drinks up 41.2% on the Kazakhstan PepsiCo licence. Western Europe grew organic revenue 2.5% on volume of +0.3%. China volume fell about 10% (it fell 6% in Q2). Guidance of 4-6% organic operating profit growth for FY2026 was repeated. The expected translation effect on FY2026 operating profit was raised to +DKK 100m, from flat. From the regional figures, we work out that Asia's organic revenue fell about 2-4% in Q3. This is our inference; the company did not publish it.
  3. Basis for operating profit. Guidance and consensus use Carlsberg Performance Measures (CPM), the company's own measures, introduced with its early adoption of IFRS 18 in 2026. The restated 2025 base is DKK 13,686m. The model should use that figure, and should not use any of the others in circulation: - DKK 13,996m, the old base before IFRS 18; - DKK 11,927m, FactSet's standardised adjusted EBIT; - DKK 10,230m, IFRS 18 reported operating profit.

Consensus EBIT for FY2026 is DKK 14,472m. That equals 13,686 × 1.05 + DKK 100m of currency translation. So consensus sits exactly at the midpoint of guidance, not at the top.

  1. Basis for EPS. Use the company's adjusted FY2025 EPS of DKK 60.81 as the base. FactSet's "brokers'-basis actual" for FY2025, DKK 45.10, is the reported figure, so FactSet's "+44%" EPS growth for FY2026 is an artefact of mixing bases. Consensus (FactSet median, 28 October 2026):
Year Consensus EPS (DKK) Growth Note
FY2026 65.01 +6.9% on DKK 60.81
FY2027 69.25 +6.5%
FY2028 76.07 +9.8%
FY2029 82.73 two brokers only
  1. Basis for leverage. On the company's definition, net interest-bearing debt to EBITDA was 3.28x at end-2025 (DKK 61.6bn of net debt on EBITDA of about DKK 18.8bn) and 3.0x at H1 2026. The target is below 2.5x by end-2027. FactSet's figure for H1 2026 is 3.59x, because FactSet counts leases and, at face value, the EUR 1.8bn hybrid bond as debt. The hybrid is worth about DKK 13.4bn, or about 0.7x EBITDA. The model should choose one definition and treat the hybrid's coupon consistently with it.
  2. Items below operating profit that the model must carry: - Net finance costs, excluding currency effects, are guided at DKK 1.8bn for 2026, down from the earlier DKK 2.2bn. Part of the cut is that hybrid coupons no longer sit in finance costs. - The hybrid coupon is about DKK 475m a year after tax: roughly EUR 83m gross, at coupons of 4.37% and 4.875%, after 23% tax. It is deducted for EPS in full from 2027 and in part in 2026. - Tax is guided at about 23%; the reported rate for 2025 was 24.7%. - Sapporo will own 25% of the Southeast Asia and Hong Kong joint venture from closing, expected at end-2026. On our arithmetic, that minority takes about USD 30m of EBIT a year, about DKK 150m of net income. Bernstein's DKK 40m looks too low. - Profits from associates (about DKK 660m after tax in 2025) now sit below operating profit under IFRS 18. - The consensus share count is flat at about 132m, so consensus includes no share buyback.
  3. Valuation. Price to consensus earnings 24 months forward is 12.9x at DKK 960.6 (28 October) and about 12.5x at DKK 934.2. The comparisons: - five-year range 9.95x to 18.83x, median 13.80x; - ten-year median 16.9x; ten-year 10th percentile 13.2x (the dashboard puts today at the 8th percentile of ten years); - two-year median 11.89x.

The memo's re-rating leg, from 11.33x to a blended 12.8x, has largely happened already. It came in an October sector rotation without support from estimates or target prices.

  1. Calendar: - Sapporo joint venture closes by end-2026 (USD 643m). - Kazakhstan production moves in-house by end-2026; the plant opened on 1 October 2026. - Azerbaijan PepsiCo licence starts 1 January 2027. - FY2026 results on 9 February 2027: FY2027 guidance, leverage, and the buyback decision. - UK deposit return scheme starts 1 October 2027. - The UK sugar levy threshold falls to 4.5g per 100ml on 1 January 2028. - The Coca-Cola bottling contracts in Denmark and Finland end on 31 December 2028. - PepsiCo bottling in Denmark, Finland and the Baltics starts 1 January 2029. - India listing: draft documents were filed confidentially on 2 July 2026, and DNB Carnegie reported the flotation postponed on 9 October 2026. A draft prospectus dated 27 October 2026 also appears among the Change Forces citations. The status is unresolved, so treat the listing as optional, not as part of the base case.
  2. Steer for the model. This is a self-help and deleveraging case with no growth in demand. The model should assume: - underlying volume flat to +1%; - price and mix of about 2-3% in 2027, enough to cover a cost headwind of about 160bp; - organic operating profit growth of 5-7% a year; - EPS growth of about 8-9% a year over FY2026-FY2028 before any buyback, and 1-3 points more once buybacks restart. The restart is more likely with the H1 2027 results than in February 2027.

The swing factors are China, input costs in 2027, how much leaks out below operating profit (the hybrid coupon, minorities and associates), and when the buyback restarts.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Carlsberg A/S (CARL.B-DK), a Danish brewer and multi-beverage company, which also bottles soft drinks under PepsiCo licence. It reports three regions: Western Europe (including Britvic soft drinks since January 2025), Asia (China through Chongqing Brewery, Vietnam, Laos, Malaysia, and the Southeast Asia and Hong Kong joint venture with Sapporo), and Central and Eastern Europe and India (including PepsiCo bottling in Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan and, from 2027, Azerbaijan).

Request: T-P1

Peers:

  • Heineken (closest global brewer peer; EverGreen 2030 cost programme and European restructuring)
  • AB InBev (scale and cost benchmark; margin recovery after the SABMiller deal)
  • Royal Unibrew (Nordic multi-beverage brewer; fixed-cost absorption as it loses its PepsiCo licences in 2029)
  • Coca-Cola HBC and Coca-Cola Europacific Partners (soft-drink bottlers; cost structure and operating leverage of bottling)
  • Molson Coors (European mainstream beer and cost cutting)
  • Asahi Group (Central European beer; Polish market)
  • China Resources Beer and Tsingtao (Chinese brewery closures, capacity and utilisation)
  • Mattoni 1873 (private PepsiCo bottler in Central Europe; bottling cost benchmark)

Inputs:

  • What share of Carlsberg's cost base is fixed: brewery and bottling depreciation, logistics, overheads and marketing? How did operating profit move with volume in the 2020 COVID downturn, the 2022-23 cost inflation, the 2025 organic volume decline of 2.0%, and the Chinese volume declines of Q2 and Q3 2026?
  • What did "Funding the Journey" (2015-19; DKK 3bn of savings against a DKK 2bn target, per Jefferies) actually deliver to the operating margin, and over what period? Which cost lines proved cuttable?
  • How are the Britvic cost synergies of GBP 110m (company, October 2025) delivering by bucket: overhead, procurement, logistics, supply chain? Are they net or gross of reinvestment, with what integration cost (GBP 83m disclosed), and what is left after 2026?
  • How much margin did third-party toll-filling cost in Kazakhstan in 2026? What margin do comparable bottlers earn once production is in-house, given the Almaty plant opened on 1 October 2026?
  • How much under-absorption of fixed costs follows peak-season volume falls in Chinese breweries (Chongqing Brewery disclosures; Morgan Stanley's operating-deleverage commentary)?
  • What do the restructurings of Teisseire (France), Norway, Cambodia, China, Germany and the UK cost, and what do they save, against special items of DKK 1,926m in 2025?
  • What are the cost targets in the Accelerate SAIL framework (flattish cost of goods sold per hectolitre in 2026, G&A flat as a share of revenue, marketing towards 8.5-9% of revenue), and what is the track record against them?
  • What did Heineken's EverGreen savings (EUR 400-500m a year) deliver to margin after reinvestment? What happened to Royal Unibrew's fixed-cost absorption after past licence changes?

Focus: How much of Carlsberg's operating profit growth in 2026-29 can come from cost (the remaining Britvic synergies, ending Kazakhstan toll-filling, Teisseire and supply-chain programmes) rather than volume. And how sensitive is operating profit, given the fixed-cost structure, to a renewed fall in Chinese or Western European volume?

Request: T-P2

Peers:

  • Heineken (premium beer pricing in Europe and Asia; pricing slightly below inflation in Europe)
  • AB InBev (revenue per hectolitre growth across cycles)
  • Royal Unibrew (Nordic pricing; own-brand soft drinks)
  • China Resources Beer, Tsingtao and Budweiser APAC (Chinese premium and mainstream pricing)
  • Sabeco (Vietnamese pricing under excise increases)
  • United Breweries (Indian state-regulated pricing)
  • Coca-Cola Europacific Partners (UK soft-drink pricing against Carlsberg Britvic)
  • Asahi Group and Molson Coors (Polish and UK mainstream price competition)

Operating conditions:

  • input cost inflation (aluminium, energy, glass), including the 2022-23 spike and the cost rise forecast for 2027;
  • consumer downturns and down-trading (China 2025-26; UK and Polish household squeeze);
  • excise and sugar-tax increases (Vietnam's special consumption tax roadmap; sugar levies in Poland, Italy and the UK);
  • deposit return scheme introductions (Poland 2026, UK October 2027, Austria precedent);
  • retailer negotiations and delistings in concentrated European grocery;
  • currency devaluation in emerging markets (Laos, Ukraine, Kazakhstan).

Inputs:

  • How did Carlsberg's organic price and mix and revenue per hectolitre behave by region in each condition above? Recent figures: +0.8% in Q1 2026, +1.0% in H1 2026, and revenue per hectolitre +3% in Q3 2026 (company).
  • How much of the Q3 2026 rise in revenue per hectolitre was pure price, how much brand and category mix, and how much country mix from lower Chinese volume?
  • How quickly is excise passed through? What was the volume elasticity after past Vietnamese, Malaysian and Polish tax increases?
  • What evidence is there that the stated 2027 policy holds: "price in line with local CPI" and "cover over 80% of input inflation through revenue management" (Bernstein, 9 September 2026)? How much price is needed to offset aluminium and energy (Bernstein: about 1.3% for aluminium; Jefferies: about 1% for a 10% energy rise)?
  • What is the pricing record for the premium Carlsberg brand, 1664 Blanc in China, Tuborg in India, and local power brands (Feldschlösschen, Chongqing, Huda, Beerlao)?
  • How are PepsiCo's concentrate pricing and pass-through terms structured for bottlers? Coca-Cola system soft-drink pricing in the UK is the comparison.
  • What is the evidence of price given back or volume lost: the Swiss retailer dispute in Q4 2025, Polish mainstream volume, UK on-trade share after Heineken and Molson Coors cut prices on lower-strength mainstream lagers?
  • How do Heineken's and AB InBev's price and mix in Q3 2026 compare, and why?

Focus: Whether Carlsberg can raise prices enough in 2027 to cover the expected rise in input costs without further volume loss, and whether its pricing is real or nominal. And how much of recent revenue-per-hectolitre growth is pricing rather than mix.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Carlsberg's 2026-29 earnings depend on several linked dynamics that could compound or stall together, so the second set needs evidence on how such loops have run in practice.

Peers:

  • AB InBev (deleveraging that led to buybacks and a re-rating, 2025-26)
  • Heineken (the China Resources Beer partnership and share gains in premium)
  • Royal Unibrew (the reverse loop: licence loss leading to lost scale)
  • Coca-Cola HBC (bottler density and franchise expansion)
  • Budweiser APAC (the channel shift in China from on-premise to off-premise)
  • Diageo (premium price reset and its volume response)

Inputs:

  • Deleveraging, then lower interest, then a buyback restart, then EPS accretion and a re-rating. Precedents: AB InBev 2023-26, and Carlsberg itself in 2015-19. What stops this loop?
  • Multi-beverage density. Each PepsiCo licence lowers the cost to serve the next, which makes PepsiCo award more territories. Precedents: the Nordic model since about 2000; Kazakhstan, Azerbaijan, Georgia and Armenia; the Danish, Finnish and Baltic licences from 2029. What reverses it (Royal Unibrew's licence loss; Carlsberg's own San Miguel and Coca-Cola contract exits)?
  • Chinese volume falls, then distributor destocking, then brewery under-absorption, then more marketing spend and pressure on premium share. Precedents: Budweiser APAC 2024-26, Chongqing Brewery. What breaks this loop?
  • Soft-drink mix growth dilutes revenue per hectolitre and gross margin while improving route density. Does the net effect on margin compound or reverse (Redburn's estimate of 30bp a year of mix drag)?
  • A cost-discipline culture moves to incentives weighted to revenue growth. Is there evidence from peers that such shifts erode cost discipline (marketing ratio, special items) or lift growth?

Focus: Which of these loops are operating now, with dated evidence, and which are most likely to strengthen or stall in 2027. Above all: does deleveraging lead to buybacks and a re-rating, and does Chinese weakness feed operating deleverage?

Request: T-P4

Why requested: The bridge from Carlsberg's operating profit to EPS changes materially in 2026-28 and is modelled unevenly by the sell-side. FactSet shows FY2027 EPS estimates from DKK 65.57 to DKK 75.00 on revenue estimates close to one another.

Peers:

  • Heineken (EPS bridge with hybrids, associates and minorities)
  • AB InBev (EPS accretion from deleveraging and buybacks)
  • Royal Unibrew (tax and financing at a Danish beverage company)
  • Coca-Cola HBC (minorities and joint ventures in a bottler)

Inputs:

  • The EUR 1.8bn hybrid issued in May 2026: coupons of 4.37% and 4.875% and non-call periods of 5.25 and 8 years. How is the coupon treated in adjusted EPS? Jefferies estimates about DKK 475m a year; Morgan Stanley about 2% FY2027 EPS dilution; Bernstein about 1%. What are the step-up terms and the equity credit from the rating agencies?
  • Net finance costs. What drives the cut in 2026 guidance from DKK 2.2bn to DKK 1.8bn (excluding currency effects), and how do they evolve with net debt into 2027-28?
  • The Sapporo joint venture: Sapporo pays USD 643m for 25% of Southeast Asia and Hong Kong, at 21.3x 2025 EBIT (Morgan Stanley). What is the expected minority charge and closing date? Compare Bernstein's estimate of the net effect on EPS with alternative calculations.
  • Tax rate guidance (about 23% for 2026) against 24.7% reported in 2025, and what drives it.
  • Associates under IFRS 18 (Super Bock, the Chinese associates): their contribution and its trend.
  • The restated 2025 operating profit base of DKK 13,686m under Carlsberg Performance Measures, against IFRS 18 reported operating profit. What is excluded, including amortisation of intangibles from the Britvic purchase?
  • Buybacks. When does management expect to restart, and how large would a programme be, with net debt to EBITDA below 2.5x? Brokers' views range from February 2027 (Jefferies) to H2 2027 (DNB Carnegie, 9 October 2026).
  • The Indian listing. What is its status after reports of a postponement (DNB Carnegie, 9 October 2026)? What are the expected proceeds, the minority charge and the accretion?
  • The share count, dilution, and the dividend payout policy (about 50% of adjusted net profit).

Focus: How the items between operating profit and EPS move in 2026-28: finance costs, the hybrid coupon, the Sapporo and other minorities, associates, tax, and the share count. And how far the sell-side's treatment of each differs, so EPS can be built line by line from operating profit.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 30 October 2026, 05:11 to 05:32 UK, Opus, richie login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Carlsberg (CARL.B-DK): answers to the second set of questions (Financial forecasts)

Written 30 October 2026 for the Investment Analyst who answers the third and fourth sets. Share price DKK 934.2 (29 October 2026). All money in DKK millions unless stated. "EBIT" means operating profit on Carlsberg Performance Measures (CPM), the basis of guidance and consensus; "EPS" means the company's adjusted EPS (FY2025 base DKK 60.81). "Leverage" means the company's net interest-bearing debt (NIBD) to EBITDA.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

The six judgements that matter most:

  1. EPS compounds at about 7.5-8% a year (FY2025-FY2030: 60.81 to 89.92, 8.1% a year), almost exactly at consensus in level, but with a different composition. Our revenue is about 0.5 points a year below consensus and our EBIT is at consensus for FY2026-27 and about 1% below it in FY2028. Below EBIT we carry larger finance-cost savings than consensus appears to, larger minority interests, and a buyback from H2 2027, which consensus omits. The buyback is what holds FY2028 EPS at consensus.
  2. FY2027 is the pinch year. EPS growth dips to about 6%: the full-year hybrid coupon (-2.0 points), the Sapporo minority and Asian recovery flowing to minorities (-1.7 points), 2027 input costs, and the Kazakhstan volume lap all land in one year. Finance savings (+2.5 points) and the last synergies offset most of that. FY2028 re-accelerates to about 9% because the one-off steps drop out and the buyback starts to count.
  3. Self-help is front-loaded and largely exhausted by end-2027. Named cost and perimeter levers (Britvic synergies, Kazakhstan in-housing, restructurings) supply more than half of EBIT growth in FY2026 and FY2027 and about one-sixth in FY2028. From FY2028, EBIT growth of about 5% has to come from price and mix running ahead of costs, which is the company's historical underlying rate (about 3% a year) plus buyback and India optionality. That is why we hold EBIT growth at 5% rather than the "high-single-digit compounding" management has floated.
  4. Pricing is defensive, not real. Price and mix of about 2.5% in 2027 covers roughly 85-95% of cost inflation in absolute terms. Gross margin is flat to slightly down. EBIT margin rises about 30bp a year only because of synergies, Kazakhstan, logistics density and overhead control.
  5. The hybrid moved cost, it did not remove it. Net finance costs fall from about DKK 1.95bn (2026, all-in) to about DKK 1.65bn (2027), but the hybrid coupon of DKK 620m gross (DKK 477m after tax) now sits below net income. Net of the coupon, financing adds only about half a point to EPS growth in each of FY2026 and FY2027, and the total cost of debt plus hybrid barely falls after 2027. From 2027 the EPS benefit of the balance sheet comes through the share count, not through interest.
  6. Width and skew. FY2027 EPS range about DKK 67 to 72 around 69.5, skewed slightly down in the operating lines (costs, China) and slightly up below them (buyback timing, India listing). FY2028 range about DKK 70 to 81 around 76, roughly symmetric.

Where we disagree with the first set, and why:

  • EPS growth. The first set steered to 8-9% a year before buybacks plus 1-3 points from buybacks (that is, 9-12%). Our build gives about 6% a year before buybacks and about 7.5-8% including them. The gap comes from: EBIT growth of about 5% rather than 5-7%; minority interests that are larger and rising; and the hybrid coupon consuming most of the interest saving. The memo's modal 9.2% a year (FY2026-29) is about 1.5 points a year above our base.
  • Minority interests are larger than the first set's decomposition implies. The first set put "minorities plus hybrid" at about DKK 0.9bn in FY2026, implying minorities of about DKK 0.6bn. Reconciling the company's FY2025 adjusted net profit of DKK 8,060m with CPM EBIT of DKK 13,686m, associates of about DKK 660m, finance costs consistent with the DKK 1.8bn 2026 guide and tax of about 23-24% only works if minorities are about DKK 1.1-1.2bn. That fits Carlsberg owning only part of its big Asian subsidiaries (the listed Chongqing Brewery, Lao Brewery, Carlsberg Malaysia), and cash dividends to minorities of DKK 821m in 2025. This is our reconstruction, not a disclosed figure. It is the least-evidenced line in the EPS bridge, and the Consumer should check it against the 2025 annual report.
  • The hybrid and leverage. The first set said FactSet's 3.59x exceeds the company's 3.0x because FactSet counts leases and the full hybrid. Our reading of the numbers is different. The company's NIBD already includes leases: FactSet and company net debt matched at end-2024 (about DKK 26.5bn, 1.73x) and end-2025 (about DKK 61bn). The H1 2026 gap of about DKK 6.7bn equals half the hybrid. That fits management's statement on the Q2 2026 call that it aligns with the rating agencies' 50% debt treatment. The other driver is FactSet's lower EBITDA denominator. So the company's 3.0x already carries half the hybrid as debt. It is less flattered than the first set suggests.
  • Free cash flow. The first set called consensus free cash flow "generous". On FactSet's definition (operating cash flow less capital expenditure, before leases), our figures are at or slightly above consensus: about DKK 8.2bn, 9.5bn and 10.5bn for FY2026-28, against 7.8bn, 9.4bn and 10.0bn. The real point is definitional. FactSet's figure is about DKK 2.5-2.7bn a year above what is distributable to Carlsberg's shareholders, because of leases (about DKK 1.0bn), the hybrid coupon (DKK 0.6bn) and minority dividends (about DKK 0.9-1.1bn).
  • Revenue. The first set used 3.5-4%. We use about 3% organic and 3.1-3.3% reported. The ten-year organic rate is 3.1%, Kazakhstan laps in 2027, China recovery dilutes revenue per hectolitre, soft drinks take about 30bp a year off mix, and Jefferies (2.3%) and Redburn (3.0%) sit below consensus (3.7-3.8%).

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Revenue growth (Part 1) = organic about 3.0% (2026), 2.9% (2027), 3.2% (2028), 2.8% (2029), 3.2% (2030), plus currency and perimeter:
  • +0.3 points in 2026 (translation, Georgia and Armenia);
  • +0.2 in 2027;
  • +0.3 in 2029 (net revenue gain from the Nordic licence swap).
  • Organic revenue = volume (including licence takeovers) of about +0.8%, +0.5%, +0.6%, +0.3%, +0.6%, plus revenue per hectolitre of about +2.0-2.3%. Pure price is about 2.5% a year (local CPI plus excise pass-through). Soft-drink mix is about -0.3 points a year, and China's recovery dilutes revenue per hectolitre by about 0.2-0.3 points in 2027.
  • Half-years (Part 3):
  • H2 2026: revenue +4.0% on organic about 3% plus translation of about +1%. H1 2026 had an extra Britvic fortnight (consolidated from 16-Jan-25), which H2 does not.
  • H1 2027: +2.5%. The Kazakhstan volume lap and Chinese comparatives that are not yet easy in Q1 make this the soft half.
  • H2 2027: +3.8%, on easy Chinese comparatives (Q3 2026 China volume about -10%).
  • EBITDA margins: 21.6%, 21.5%, 21.8%. The H1 2026 actual on the same basis was about 21.1%; H2 2025 about 21.1%.
  • EBITDA margin. EBITDA is CPM EBIT plus D&A excluding acquired-intangible amortisation (FY2025 brokers' basis 18,721, 21.0%). EBIT grows:
  • +6.5% in 2026 (organic +5.75%, translation +DKK 100m);
  • +5.0% in 2027: synergies +190, Kazakhstan +200, China +100, India +90, underlying price over cost net of the marketing step-up +150;
  • +5.0% in 2028;
  • +4.0% in 2029 (Nordic swap);
  • +5.0% in 2030.
  • D&A rises with capex of DKK 6.3-6.5bn a year (new plants in Kazakhstan, Azerbaijan, India; Nordic PepsiCo capacity).
  • Net financial items combine three things:
  • net finance costs including currency and other financial items: -1,950, -1,650, -1,600, -1,600, -1,600;
  • share of associates (Super Bock, Chinese associates; below EBIT under IFRS 18): +600, +620, +640, +660, +680;
  • the hybrid coupon, gross: -390 (2026, accrued from the May 2026 issue), then -620 a year. EUR 1.8bn at 4.37% and 4.875%, about DKK 477m after tax. It is classified as equity but deducted for EPS. If the model deducts the hybrid coupon separately, remove it from this line.
  • Tax rate = 23% on pre-tax profit including associates (company guide about 23%; H1 2026 22.7%; 2025 reported 24.7%).
  • Minority interests, not a form line. About -1,180, -1,340, -1,390, -1,430, -1,480 (DKK m). These include the Sapporo 25% of Southeast Asia and Hong Kong from 2027 (about DKK 150m). If the model carries no minority line, or a small one, its EPS will be about DKK 9-11 too high. The size of minorities is our reconstruction from the FY2025 adjusted EPS of DKK 60.81; the company does not guide it.
  • Dividend per share is the dividend declared for each year, at a 48-50% payout of adjusted EPS (FY2025 DKK 29.00, 48%).
  • Share count. No dilution: incentive shares come from treasury. Buybacks in Part 2: DKK 2bn in H2 2027 (announcement expected with H1 2027 results), then DKK 4bn, 4.5bn and 5bn, at assumed prices of DKK 1,000-1,220. Average shares: 132.4m, 131.9m, 128.5m, 124.6m, 120.6m. Year-end 2030: about 118.5m.
  • Working capital uses FactSet's definition: receivables plus inventories less payables, as a share of last-twelve-month revenue (end-2025 -13.5%; end-2024 -16.5%). We assume gradual erosion as supplier finance (DKK 3.2bn) normalises.
  • Lease payments are our estimate (not disclosed in the sources): about DKK 1.0bn a year, rising with Britvic's logistics estate.
  • Acquisitions less disposals:
  • 2026: +4,180 Sapporo proceeds (USD 643m; closing expected by end-2026) less about 615 Georgia and Armenia bottlers (paid in H1) and about 100 of other items.
  • 2027-30: about -300 a year of bolt-ons.
  • If Sapporo closes in 2027, move the +4,180 to FY2027.
  • The India listing (secondary sale, up to USD 700m) is not in the inputs.
  • Restructuring cash (outside CPM EBIT) is about -800, -500, -400, -300, -250. It is included in our free-cash-flow cross-check.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

Guided item (FY2026, as repeated 29-Oct-26) Guidance Our input Comment

Translation effect on operating profit About +DKK 100m (raised from flat) +DKK 100m
Operating profit (CPM) Implied DKK 14,330-14,610 on a base of DKK 13,686 14,570 Consensus 14,472 (exactly the midpoint)
Net finance costs excluding currency About DKK 1.8bn (cut from 2.2bn) 1.8bn excluding currency; 1.95bn including currency and other
Tax rate About 23% 23%
Capex DKK 6-7bn DKK 6.5bn

Mid-term framework (Accelerate SAIL; capital markets day October 2025):

  • Organic revenue of 4-6% a year: our 2.9-3.2% is below it. The target has never been met; the ten-year record is 3.1%.
  • Operating profit growing faster than revenue: met (about 5% against about 3%). Management's informal "high-single-digit operating profit compounding" is above our 5%.
  • Gross margin back to 47-49% pro forma: not reached in our inputs (about 45.5% throughout).
  • Marketing at 8.5-9% of revenue: reached gradually (8.5% by 2028).
  • Leverage below 2.5x by end-2027: about 2.5x at end-2026, about 2.4x at end-2027.
  • Dividend payout about 50%: met.
  • Buybacks to resume below 2.5x: from H2 2027.

FY2027 guidance is due 9-Feb-27. We expect a wide initial range of about 2-6% or 3-7% organic EBIT, and our 5.0% to sit in its upper half.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Operating profit. Guidance and consensus use CPM (IFRS 18, early-adopted in 2026). FY2025 base DKK 13,686m. That differs from:
  • DKK 13,996m, the old pre-IFRS 18 basis, which included associates and excluded bank fees and trade-loan items;
  • IFRS 18 reported operating profit, DKK 10,230m, which is after amortisation of acquired intangibles (about DKK 640m a year, mainly Britvic: brands DKK 4.4bn, contract-related assets DKK 9.3bn) and items previously called special (DKK 1,926m in 2025);
  • FactSet standardised adjusted EBIT, DKK 11,927m, which is not comparable.
  • Consensus FY2026 EBIT of DKK 14,472m = 13,686 × 1.05 + 100.
  • EBITDA. The brokers' basis (FY2025 DKK 18,721m, 21.0%) is approximately CPM EBIT plus D&A excluding acquired-intangible amortisation. FactSet's operating EBITDA (DKK 17,355m, 19.5%) is a different, lower basis; do not compute growth across the two. Our EBITDA inputs are on the brokers' basis.
  • EPS. Use the company's adjusted EPS (FY2025 DKK 60.81, adjusted net profit DKK 8,060m). It excludes acquired-intangible amortisation and items previously called special, and deducts minorities and (from 2026) the hybrid coupon. FactSet's "brokers' basis actual" of DKK 45.10 is reported EPS, so FactSet's +44% FY2026 growth is a basis artefact. On the company basis, consensus growth is +6.9% (FY2026), +6.5% (FY2027) and +9.8% (FY2028).
  • Below EBIT under IFRS 18. Associates sit below operating profit. The hybrid coupon sits below net income, not in finance costs. Brokers place both unevenly: FY2027 broker EPS runs from DKK 65.57 to 75.00 on revenue within about 4%.
  • Net debt and leverage. The company's NIBD includes lease liabilities (FactSet and the company matched at end-2024 and end-2025). On our reading of the H1 2026 numbers, it also includes 50% of the EUR 1.8bn hybrid, in line with the rating agencies' equity credit. FactSet's 3.59x at H1 2026 counts 100% of the hybrid and uses FactSet's lower operating EBITDA; the company's figure is 3.0x.
  • Free cash flow. FactSet's free cash flow (consensus 7,789, 9,356, 10,000 for FY2026-28) is operating cash flow less capex, before lease payments. Our cross-check figure deducts lease payments (about DKK 1.0bn), so add about 1.0-1.1bn to compare it with FactSet's. Neither figure deducts the hybrid coupon (DKK 0.62bn) or minority dividends (about DKK 0.9-1.1bn), which together make distributable cash about DKK 1.5-1.7bn lower again.
  • Our cross-check definitions:
  • EPS = (CPM EBIT + net financial items as defined above) × (1 - 23%) - minorities, divided by average shares.
  • Free cash flow = EBITDA - cash tax - net finance costs (excluding the hybrid) - change in working capital - capex - lease payments - restructuring cash + dividends from associates. It is before the hybrid coupon, minority dividends, dividends, M&A and buybacks.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Carlsberg A/S (CARL.B-DK), a Danish brewer and multi-beverage company that also bottles PepsiCo soft drinks under licence; three reporting regions: Western Europe (including Britvic soft drinks since January 2025), Asia (China through the listed Chongqing Brewery, Vietnam, Laos, Malaysia, Cambodia and the Southeast Asia and Hong Kong joint venture in which Sapporo is buying 25%), and Central and Eastern Europe and India (including PepsiCo bottling in Kazakhstan and Kyrgyzstan and, from 2027, Azerbaijan)

Request: T-D1

Peers: Heineken (closest global brewer; EverGreen 2030 cost programme; China through China Resources Beer); AB InBev (scale and margin benchmark; deleveraging followed by a buyback restart, 2023-26); Royal Unibrew (Nordic multi-beverage brewer losing its PepsiCo licences at end-2028); Coca-Cola HBC (bottler expanding territories and integrating acquisitions); Coca-Cola Europacific Partners (UK soft-drink competitor to Carlsberg Britvic; bottler cost structure); Molson Coors (European mainstream beer; cost programme of USD 450m over 2026-28); Asahi Group (Polish and Central European beer); China Resources Beer, Tsingtao and Budweiser APAC (Chinese volume, channel and premium dynamics); Diageo (premium price reset and USD 1bn savings programme); Sapporo (joint-venture partner in Southeast Asia) Operating conditions: input-cost inflation as hedges roll into 2027 (aluminium, energy, glass, PET, logistics); weak Chinese on-trade and nightlife demand with distributor destocking; European household squeeze with mainstream lager price competition (UK duty by alcohol strength, Polish deposit return scheme from January 2026); escalating excise (Vietnam special consumption tax roadmap; Malaysia Budget 2026); new deposit return schemes (UK from 1 October 2027) and sugar levies (UK threshold to 4.5g per 100ml from 1 January 2028); bottling-licence transitions (Kazakhstan in-house from October 2026, Azerbaijan from January 2027, Coca-Cola to PepsiCo in Denmark and Finland from January 2029); post-acquisition deleveraging with higher refinancing rates Inputs: (1) Carlsberg grew organic operating profit 6.0% in 2024 and 5.0% in 2025 and guides 4-6% for 2026, helped by Britvic cost synergies that the company expects to be about 80% of GBP 110m by end-2026: what sources of profit growth replaced finished cost programmes in Carlsberg's past (after Funding the Journey, 2015-19) and at peers after integration synergies ended, and how quickly did profit growth slow? (2) Carlsberg has targeted 4-6% organic revenue growth since February 2024 and delivered 2.7% in H1 2026 and 3.0% in Q3 2026: has it, or any European brewer with a similar footprint, sustained organic revenue growth at that rate, and from what mix of volume and price? (3) Carlsberg's stated 2027 pricing policy is to price in line with local inflation and cover over 80% of input inflation through revenue management (Bernstein, 9 September 2026): what is the record of Carlsberg and peers holding such policies in cost-inflation years (2011, 2022, 2023), and what volume and share were given up? (4) Can the USD 344m Kazakhstan plant (opened 1 October 2026) turn a PepsiCo business that was loss-making on toll-filling in H1 2026 (Jefferies) into one earning near group margins within a year, and what did comparable bottlers' in-sourcing achieve, and how fast? (5) What does Carlsberg's own record (2015-19) and AB InBev's (2023-26) show about how soon after reaching a leverage target buybacks restart, and at what size relative to free cash flow? (6) What record does Carlsberg have of absorbing a licence change (UK San Miguel loss in 2025; Coca-Cola's 2019 halt to Danish-German border trade), and what does that imply for the Danish and Finnish switch in January 2029? Focus: Whether Carlsberg's demonstrated strengths (conservative and reliably beaten profit guidance, fast integration, cost control, cash conversion, PepsiCo's repeated choice of it as bottler) are enough to keep operating profit growing at mid-single-digit rates in 2027-28 once Britvic synergies are exhausted and input costs rise, without the revenue growth it has not yet delivered.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Heineken (European and Asian premium beer; pricing below inflation in Europe); AB InBev (EPS bridge from deleveraging and buybacks); Royal Unibrew (Danish financing, tax and licence-loss profit sensitivity); Coca-Cola HBC (minorities and joint ventures in a bottler); Budweiser APAC, China Resources Beer and Tsingtao (dispersion of Chinese brewer outcomes in 2026); Coca-Cola Europacific Partners (UK soft-drink price and cost sensitivity); Molson Coors (mainstream European beer volume and cost); Diageo (dispersion of outcomes after a price reset) Operating conditions: input-cost spike years (2011, 2022-23) and the cost rise expected for 2027; Chinese weather, destocking and on-trade downturns (2024-26); excise and deposit-scheme shocks (Vietnam, Malaysia, Poland, UK, Austria precedent); post-acquisition deleveraging with hybrid capital, joint-venture minority sales and subsidiary listings; licence takeovers and losses in soft-drink bottling Inputs: (1) The drivers that separate Carlsberg's 2026-29 earnings from 2020-25: price and mix against 2027 input costs; Chinese volume recovery against weak 2026 comparatives (China volume about -6% in Q2 and about -10% in Q3 2026, company); the Kazakhstan in-housing and Azerbaijan licence; the last Britvic synergies; and below operating profit, the EUR 1.8bn hybrid issued May 2026 (coupons 4.37% and 4.875%), the Sapporo minority from closing (expected end-2026), net finance costs (2026 guidance DKK 1.8bn excluding currency), associates under IFRS 18, the tax rate (about 23% guided), minority interests and the timing of a buyback restart. (2) For each driver: how wide is the historical range of outcomes for Carlsberg and peers, and which one or two have decided whether 18-month forecasts were met? (3) What is the historical dispersion of Carlsberg's organic operating profit outcomes against its initial guidance range since 2016, and of its organic revenue and volume against consensus? (4) What is the historical dispersion of European brewers' EPS outcomes against consensus 18 months earlier in cost-spike years, and how often did forecasts resting mainly on synergies or financing changes hold? (5) How often have Chinese brewers' volumes rebounded fully in the year after a weather-and-destocking year, and how quickly did operating margins recover? (6) How widely do brokers' FY2027 EPS estimates for Carlsberg disperse (FactSet range DKK 65.57 to 75.00 at 28 October 2026), and which below-operating-profit lines explain the spread? Focus: Whether Carlsberg's FY2027-28 earnings depend mainly on one or two drivers (2027 price against input cost, China's recovery, the buyback date) or on many small ones, and how wide and in which direction the realistic range of FY2027 outcomes is skewed.

Request: T-I1

Peers: Heineken (Q3 2026 results 28 October 2026; FY2026 organic operating profit guidance 2-6%; new chief executive from 1 October 2026); AB InBev (Q3 2026 results and buyback progress); Royal Unibrew (Q3 2026 and 2027 outlook; PepsiCo licence loss); Coca-Cola HBC and Coca-Cola Europacific Partners (2027 cost and pricing guidance); China Resources Beer, Tsingtao, Budweiser APAC and Chongqing Brewery (Q3 2026 China read-across); Molson Coors and Asahi Group (European beer pricing and cost guidance) Operating conditions: guidance-setting for 2027 under rising input costs and weak Chinese demand; IFRS 18 adoption and new company performance measures; hybrid capital and minority-sale transactions reshaping the EPS bridge; a sector rotation into defensive stocks without estimate support (October 2026) Inputs: (1) Carlsberg's FY2026 guidance: organic operating profit growth 4-6% (narrowed from 2-6% on 19 August 2026, repeated 29 October 2026), translation about +DKK 100m, net finance costs about DKK 1.8bn excluding currency, tax about 23%, capex DKK 6-7bn, on a restated 2025 base of DKK 13,686m; FactSet median consensus FY2026 EBIT DKK 14,472m (28 October 2026). How likely is delivery at the top of the range? (2) Carlsberg's initial-guidance pattern: 2024 1-5% (delivered 6.0%), 2025 1-5% (delivered 5.0%), 2026 2-6%. What range is it likely to give for FY2027 on 9 February 2027, and where will the midpoint sit against FY2027 consensus EBIT (DKK 15,324m) and EPS (DKK 69.25)? (3) Jefferies models 2027 organic EBIT growth of 4.3% against consensus 5.7% and organic sales of 2.3% against 3.7% (October 2026); Rothschild & Co Redburn cut its 2027 organic sales forecast to 3.0%: is consensus more likely to be cut or raised, and on which lines? (4) FactSet revisions for FY2027: EPS -1% and EBITDA -2% over three and six months, buy share 59% (down 4 points in three months), at 29 October 2026: what is driving the drift? (5) How are brokers modelling the hybrid coupon, the Sapporo minority, associates below operating profit and any buyback (the consensus share count is flat at about 132m), and which would move consensus most once the company's FY2027 guidance is published? (6) What would each outcome need: a guidance raise during 2027 (China rebound, pricing above costs, Kazakhstan margin), a cut (input costs, Chinese volume, deposit scheme), or delivery? (7) The mid-term framework (4-6% organic revenue growth, operating profit growing faster, leverage below 2.5x by end-2027, gross margin of 47-49% pro forma): which parts are likely to be met, raised or reduced by 2028? Focus: Where Carlsberg's initial FY2027 guidance on 9 February 2027 is likely to sit against consensus, and whether consensus FY2027-28 EPS is more likely to be cut (input costs, China, below-the-line items) or raised (buyback restart, India listing proceeds) over the next 18 months.

Request: T-I2

Peers: Heineken; AB InBev; Royal Unibrew; Coca-Cola HBC; Coca-Cola Europacific Partners; Budweiser APAC; China Resources Beer; Diageo (each discussed by the same sector analysts, so the comparison shows what European beverage investors focus on) Operating conditions: a rotation into defensive consumer staples in October 2026 that lifted brewers 9.6-17.4% without estimate or target-price support; rising bond yields; weak Chinese consumption; a 2027 input-cost upswing; a sector trading near ten-year-low multiples Inputs: For Carlsberg: (a) the role of Britvic synergies and the Kazakhstan in-housing in 2026-27 profit growth, and what replaces them after 2027; (b) whether the hybrid coupon, now below net income rather than in finance costs, offsets the fall in net finance costs; (c) the size of minority interests in Carlsberg's earnings and how they change with the Sapporo joint venture and any Indian listing; (d) the timing and size of a buyback restart once leverage is below 2.5x; (e) whether 2027 pricing covers 2027 input costs; (f) whether China's 2026 volume fall is weather and destocking or structural share loss to Heineken and China Resources Beer; (g) the economics of the Coca-Cola to PepsiCo switch in Denmark and Finland in January 2029; (h) the Indian listing's status, size and use of proceeds; (i) the effect of soft-drink mix on revenue per hectolitre and gross margin; (j) the shift of executive incentives towards revenue growth and its effect on cost discipline; (k) the IFRS 18 and company-performance-measure restatement and the resulting differences in EPS and leverage bases across data providers. Focus: Which of these dynamics are widely discussed by Carlsberg's sell-side analysts and investors, which by only one or two, and which by no one; in particular whether the below-operating-profit items (hybrid coupon, minorities) and the post-2027 fade of synergies are understood or largely ignored.

Request: OWN-1

Question: For Carlsberg A/S (CARL.B-DK), what were the profit attributable to non-controlling interests, the share of profit of associates and joint ventures, and net financial items (split between interest, currency and other items) in 2023, 2024, 2025 and H1 2026, and how did each reconcile to the company's adjusted net profit (DKK 8,060m in 2025) and adjusted EPS (DKK 60.81)? Which subsidiaries account for the non-controlling interests (Chongqing Brewery, Lao Brewery, Carlsberg Malaysia and others), with what ownership percentages and profit contributions? How will the Sapporo Southeast Asia and Hong Kong joint venture (25% to Sapporo for USD 643m, closing expected by end-2026) and a possible Carlsberg India listing change them? How do individual sell-side brokers model each of these lines for FY2027-28?

Request: OWN-2

Question: How does Carlsberg A/S (CARL.B-DK) define net interest-bearing debt for its target of leverage below 2.5x: does it include lease liabilities, and what share of the EUR 1.8bn hybrid issued in May 2026? What are the hybrid's coupon dates, cumulative or deferral terms, treatment in adjusted EPS under IAS 33, reset and step-up terms and rating-agency equity credit? How much of the cut in 2026 net finance cost guidance (from DKK 2.2bn to DKK 1.8bn excluding currency) came from bond redemptions funded by the hybrid, and how much from IFRS 18 reclassification? What maturity and refinancing schedule, average cost of debt and currency-related financial items do company statements and brokers imply for 2027-28?

Request: OWN-3
