How much of Carlsberg's 2026 Chinese volume decline (about -6% in Q2 and about -10% in Q3, company) was weather and destocking, and how much was share loss to Heineken through China Resources Beer or to instant-retail and off-trade channels where Carlsberg is under-represented?

Request: OWN-4

Question: What cost of goods sold per hectolitre is Carlsberg A/S (CARL.B-DK) likely to face in 2027, given its hedging policy (at least 70% of barley and aluminium hedged by Q3 of the prior year, 90% at the start of the year, rolling energy hedges), current aluminium, energy, glass, PET and logistics prices, and supplier contracts? What price increases has Carlsberg announced or implemented for 2027 in its main markets (Denmark, Switzerland, the UK, Poland, China, Vietnam, Laos, Kazakhstan, Ukraine, India), and how do they compare with the 2027 cost and pricing commentary of Heineken, Royal Unibrew, Coca-Cola HBC, Coca-Cola Europacific Partners and Molson Coors?

Request: OWN-5

Question: What have Carlsberg A/S (CARL.B-DK) management and filings said, at the Q3 2026 trading statement (29 October 2026), investor meetings and in the Carlsberg India updated draft red herring prospectus (27 October 2026), about: the timing and size of a share buyback restart; the Indian listing's offer size (86.42 million shares), pricing, timing and use of proceeds; the closing of the Sapporo transaction; and the leverage the company intends to hold once below 2.5x? How have AB InBev, Heineken and Coca-Cola HBC sized buybacks against free cash flow and leverage after deleveraging?

Request: OWN-6

Question: What are the economics of Carlsberg A/S's (CARL.B-DK) PepsiCo bottling expansion? Specifically: the margin of the Kazakhstan and Kyrgyzstan business now that the Almaty plant (opened 1 October 2026) has replaced toll-filling; the expected profitability path of the Azerbaijan licence from 1 January 2027 and the Georgia and Armenia bottlers; and the switch from Coca-Cola to PepsiCo bottling in Denmark and Finland, plus the Baltics, from 1 January 2029. For the switch: the revenue, volume and profit of the expiring Coca-Cola contracts, the PepsiCo volume and share Carlsberg inherits from Royal Unibrew, transition costs, and fixed-cost absorption at Danish and Finnish plants. What precedents of bottler franchise swaps exist, and who is likely to take the Danish and Finnish Coca-Cola licences?

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 31 October 2026, 16:51 to 17:12 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Carlsberg (CARL.B-DK): answers to the third and fourth sets of questions

Written 31 October 2026 for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo. Share price DKK 934.2 (29 October 2026). Money in DKK millions unless stated. "EBIT" means operating profit on Carlsberg Performance Measures (CPM; FY2025 base DKK 13,686m); "EPS" means the company's adjusted EPS (FY2025 DKK 60.81); "leverage" means the company's net interest-bearing debt (NIBD) to EBITDA, which includes leases and 50% of the hybrid. "The model" means the Financial Analyst's model after update 1; "our base" means the second set's forecasts unless this document changes them.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Read first: the model's EPS gap, what it is, and the one input we change

1. The gap is minority interests, not a wrong operating input. The model's EPS (DKK 72.82, 80.71, 87.54, 95.12, 104.80 for FY2026-30) sits DKK 7-15 a share above the second set's cross-check (65.70, 69.52, 75.97, 82.12, 89.92). We decomposed every year. The model's net income is "before minority interests" and its EPS divides that figure by the share count, so minorities of DKK 1.18-1.48bn a year (DKK 9-12 a share) are never deducted. That single omission explains DKK 8.9, 10.2, 10.8, 11.5 and 12.3 of the five gaps. Two smaller items make up the rest. First, the model retires more shares per krone of buyback than the second set assumed: its average share count is 130.6m, 127.0m, 122.4m and 117.3m for FY2027-30 against the second set's 131.9m, 128.5m, 124.6m and 120.6m, which fits a buyback executed at about today's DKK 960 rather than the second set's rising DKK 1,000-1,220; that adds about DKK 0.7, 0.9, 1.5 and 2.5 a share in FY2027-30. Second, the model's FY2026 blends the reported H1 2026 (after special items, purchase-price amortisation and minorities) with a forecast H2, which takes about DKK 1.8 off its FY2026 figure and masks part of the minorities gap in that year. The EBIT differences between the model and the second set are within DKK 50m in every year and are rounding of the EBITDA margin inputs. The free-cash-flow gaps (model minus cross-check: +352, +122, -37, -112, -158) are small and definitional: the cross-check deducts restructuring cash (DKK 800m falling to 250m) and adds dividends from associates (about DKK 400m), which the model's lines do not separate. None of the FCF gaps needs an input change.

2. Why the gap matters more than its size suggests. Carlsberg's minorities are proportionally the largest among the European brewers: DKK 1,023m of profit attributable to non-controlling interests in FY2025, 14.65% of consolidated profit, against 11.8% at Heineken (AlphaSense own-1 report, from the 2025 annual reports). They sit in the most profitable subsidiaries (Chongqing Brewery 60% owned; Lao Brewery 61%, 28.3% EBIT margin; Carlsberg Malaysia 51%, 42.3% EBIT margin) and they rise from 2027 with the Sapporo joint venture. Left out, they inflate every EPS-based output of the model: the "two-year-forward P/E today" of 11.4x in the summary is really about 12.9x on a correct EPS (the FactSet figure), the 18-month total return of +58.1% rests on FY2029 EPS of DKK 95.12 that is 16% too high, and the three-year return of +82.3% rests on FY2030 EPS of DKK 104.80 that is also 16% too high. On our corrected EPS and the same guideline multiple of 15.27x the 18-month figure is about +38% and the three-year figure about +58%; at the five-year median multiple of 13.8x they are about +25% and +45%. The memo Analyst should explain the return on these corrected figures (topic #12), not on the model's.

3. The change we make, and why we make it this way. Richard's note (16 October 2026) says minorities and the hybrid coupon are not modelled. The hybrid coupon is in fact already in the model, because the second set folded it (gross, DKK 390m in 2026 and DKK 620m a year thereafter) into "Net financial items"; the tax rate of 23% then produces the DKK 477m after-tax cost the company will deduct. Minorities are not. The model has no line for them, and the instruction asks us to keep the model's forecasts in line with our judgement. We therefore fold the pre-tax equivalent of minority interests (minorities divided by 0.77) into "Net financial items", so that the model's net income, EPS, P/E and total return come out on the same basis as the company's adjusted EPS and consensus. This is a workaround, not a description of finance costs, and we say so in inputs-changed.md so that the Financial Analyst can reverse it if Richard prefers the model to show finance costs alone. If it is reversed, every EPS-based output of the model must be read as about 13-16% too high.

4. We also lower the second set's minority interests slightly, on new evidence. The second set reconstructed minorities at DKK 1,150m for FY2025 and DKK 1,180m for FY2026 from the adjusted EPS of DKK 60.81, assuming a 23% tax rate. The AlphaSense own-1 report now gives the actual bridge: reported net profit DKK 5,955m plus special items after tax DKK 1,624m plus purchase-price amortisation DKK 481m equals adjusted net profit DKK 8,060m, and reported minorities were DKK 1,023m (H1 2026: DKK 658m). The special items added back sat mostly in wholly owned Western European units, so adjusted-basis minorities were about DKK 1,050-1,070m, and the reconstruction then closes with an adjusted tax rate of about 24% rather than 23%. For the forecasts, tax is guided at about 23% (H1 2026: 22.7%) and China's 2026 profit is falling (Chongqing Brewery's Q3 net profit -14%), so FY2026 minorities should be nearer DKK 1,100m than 1,180m. The brokers who disclose this line sit at DKK 1,073m (DNB Carnegie, FY2026) and DKK 1,004-1,293m (FY2027), and Bernstein puts the Sapporo charge at DKK 40m against the earlier sets' DKK 150m; we use DKK 100m for Sapporo. Our minorities become DKK 1,100m, 1,280m, 1,340m, 1,390m and 1,440m (FY2026-30), DKK 40-80m below the second set. The effect is +DKK 0.6 a share in FY2026 falling to +DKK 0.3 in FY2030 (about +0.3-0.9%).

5. Our EPS after the change (second set's EBIT and share count, net financial items as changed): FY2026 DKK 66.30, FY2027 DKK 69.97, FY2028 DKK 76.36, FY2029 DKK 82.45, FY2030 DKK 90.22, against consensus of DKK 65.01, 69.25, 76.07 and 82.73 (two brokers). We are +2.0%, +1.0%, +0.4% and -0.3% against consensus. The composition still differs: revenue 0.3-1.5% below consensus, EBIT at consensus in FY2026-27 and 1.2% below in FY2028, finance costs about DKK 150-250m a year below most brokers, minorities above most brokers, and a buyback from H2 2027 that consensus omits. The model will show figures 1-3% above these from FY2027 because of its lower share count.

6. A consensus caution for the fourth set. The FactSet median FY2026 EBIT of DKK 14,472m equals the guidance midpoint (5% organic plus DKK 100m of currency). Deutsche Bank (28 September 2026) reported the Bloomberg consensus at 5.8% organic growth, which equals about DKK 14,580m, at our own 14,570 and near the top of the range. The two consensus figures differ by DKK 110m (0.8%), probably because FactSet still carries some pre-restatement bases. The first set's "about 1% upside to FY2026 EBIT against consensus" is right against FactSet and close to nil against Bloomberg. We treat the FY2026 beat as a sentiment confirmation, not a source of upgrades.

7. FactSet's broker table has its known column shift: the rows labelled Rothschild & Co Redburn, Bernstein and ABG Sundal Collier repeat the Barclays, Jyske Bank and DNB Carnegie figures. The own-1 report gives the real figures: FY2027 adjusted EPS of DKK 75.00 (DNB Carnegie), 75.79 (Bernstein), 71.52 (Deutsche Bank), 68.57 (Jefferies), 68.50 (Rothschild & Co Redburn), 68.14 (Morgan Stanley), 67.10 (Nordea), 65.57 (an unnamed broker).

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: Carlsberg A/S (CARL.B-DK), a Danish brewer and multi-beverage company that also bottles PepsiCo soft drinks under licence; three reporting regions: Western Europe (including Britvic soft drinks since January 2025), Asia (China through the 60%-owned listed Chongqing Brewery, Vietnam, Laos, Malaysia, Cambodia and the Southeast Asia and Hong Kong joint venture in which Sapporo is buying 25%), and Central and Eastern Europe and India (including PepsiCo bottling in Kazakhstan and Kyrgyzstan and, from 2027, Azerbaijan).

Request: OWN-1

Question: For Carlsberg A/S (CARL.B-DK), what exactly is the perimeter of the Southeast Asia and Hong Kong joint venture in which Sapporo Breweries agreed on 6 July 2026 to buy a 25% stake for USD 643m at 21.3x 2025 EBIT (which legal entities and countries are contributed, and whether the 61%-owned Lao Brewery and the 51%-owned Carlsberg Brewery Malaysia are inside it), what 2025 revenue, EBIT, net profit and existing non-controlling interests those contributed entities had, and what profit attributable to Sapporo the structure therefore produces from closing (expected in Q4 2026)? Carlsberg's 2025 annual report (4 February 2026) shows profit attributable to non-controlling interests of DKK 1,023m and the H1 2026 interim report (19 August 2026) DKK 658m; what do Chongqing Brewery's, Carlsberg Brewery Malaysia's and Lao Brewery's own 2026 filings and guidance imply for Carlsberg's total non-controlling interests in 2026 and 2027, how do the brokers who publish this line (DNB Carnegie, Bernstein, Rothschild & Co Redburn, Morgan Stanley, Jefferies, Deutsche Bank) model it and the Sapporo charge, and how does each of them treat the EUR 1.8bn hybrid's coupon (issued 18 May 2026 at 4.37% and 4.875%, presented below net income under IFRS 18) in its adjusted EPS for 2027 and 2028?

Request: OWN-2

Question: How has Carlsberg A/S's (CARL.B-DK) forward price-to-earnings multiple moved, in absolute terms and relative to Heineken, AB InBev, Royal Unibrew and the European consumer staples sector, through its two balance-sheet cycles: 2015-2019, when net debt to EBITDA fell from 2.34x to 1.25x and share buybacks restarted in 2017 (DKK 4.1bn repurchased in 2019 per the 2019 annual report), and 2023-2026, when the Russian exit, the Britvic acquisition (completed January 2025, buybacks suspended from July 2024) and the Chinese downturn coincided with a sector de-rating; what happened to the multiples of AB InBev (USD 6bn buyback announced 30 October 2025 at 2.87x leverage), Heineken (EUR 1.5bn buyback from February 2024; 2.5x target reached in 2014) and Carlsberg itself in the twelve months after each buyback restart following a deleveraging; how much of Carlsberg's share-price movement on a rolling six-month basis in 2025-26 is explained by the European beverage sector (including the October 2026 defensive rotation that lifted brewers 9.6-17.4%) and how much by company-specific news (for example the moves on the H1 2026 results on 19 August 2026 and the Q3 trading statement on 29 October 2026, compared with Heineken's moves on its own results on adjacent days); and which brokers publish beta or correlation estimates for Carlsberg against the sector and what they show?

Request: OWN-3

Question: What do investors, as distinct from sell-side analysts, currently say and do about Carlsberg A/S (CARL.B-DK): what is the investor commentary on X (Twitter), Seeking Alpha, fund letters, interviews and conference transcripts since the H1 2026 results (19 August 2026) and the Q3 2026 trading statement (29 October 2026) on the Britvic integration, the China volume decline (about -10% in Q3 2026 per the company), the May 2026 EUR 1.8bn hybrid, the expected resumption of share buybacks once leverage is below 2.5x, the Carlsberg India draft red herring prospectus filed on 27 October 2026 (86.42m shares offered for sale), and the Coca-Cola to PepsiCo bottling switch in Denmark and Finland from 1 January 2029; which large shareholders have increased or reduced their holdings in 2026 according to public filings, what is the short interest and securities-lending data for the B shares and how has it changed, how did the shareholder register and short interest move around the Britvic announcement (July 2024) and the Russian seizure (July 2023) as precedents; and what do investors in the European beverage sector say they are paying for in late 2026 (volume momentum, buybacks, defensiveness against rising bond yields) and what they are avoiding?
